### Restated to follow a correction above

No error of their own: these results use a corrected result and are restated accordingly.

- **Corollary 3.6.** Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, further suppose that \begin{align*} R_p(n,\delta)&=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2}),\qquad R_g(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2}),\\ R_\alpha(n,\delta)&=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-c_\alpha}) \end{align*} for some $c_\alpha>0$. Suppose in addition that the conditions of Proposition~\ref{prop:id} hold. Let $\mathcal T$ consist of the training split and independent auxiliary data. Suppose that $\hat p$, $\hat g$ and $\hat\alpha_n$ are $\mathcal T$-measurable and are the same functions and exponent for every input threshold. Write $R_U=\|U\|$, $S_U=U/\|U\|$, $\gamma=1/\beta$, $\pi_t=\mathbb P(R_U>t)$, and \[ m_d^t(x,v)=\mathbb E[Y/R_U^\alpha\mid X=x,D=d,S_U=v,R_U>t]. \] In each case below, let $\kappa$ be the exponent function specified in that case. Use fixed constants $0<c_-\leq c_+<\infty$ in the threshold order, so that $c_-n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}\leq t_n\leq c_+n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}$, and define the deterministic band \[ \ell_n=(c_-/2)n^{\kappa(\gamma)},\qquad u_n=2c_+n^{\kappa(\gamma)},\qquad I_n=[\ell_n,u_n]. \] Suppose that with probability at least $1-\delta/6$, jointly for every $t\in I_n$, \[ \begin{aligned} |\hat\alpha_n-\alpha|&\leq R_\alpha(n,\delta/6),\\ |p(x)-\hat p(x)|&\leq R_p(n/2,\delta/6),\\ |m_d^t(x,v)-\hat g(x,d,v)|&\leq R_g(n_t,\delta/6), \end{aligned} \] simultaneously almost surely over the covariate and conditional direction support and both treatments, where $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$ is the training exceedance count. On this same event suppose that the fitted-exponent pseudo-outcome has finite conditional absolute mean for every $t\in I_n$ and that, for a constant $V<\infty$ independent of $n$ and $t$, \[ \mathbb E[\psi^2\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\leq V\qquad(t\in I_n), \] where \[ \psi=\hat g(X,1,S_U)-\hat g(X,0,S_U)+\frac{D-\hat p(X)}{\hat p(X)(1-\hat p(X))}\left(\frac{Y}{R_U^{\hat\alpha_n}}-\hat g(X,D,S_U)\right). \] Set the first-factor estimate to zero if there are no evaluation exceedances. Run the full-input Hill selection of Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} at tolerance $\delta/6$, and put \[ b_n=C_{\delta/6}\begin{cases} \left(\dfrac{\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&0<\beta<1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log n\,\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&\beta=1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/(2+\beta)},&\beta>1, \end{cases} \] with the constant supplied by Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}. Define \[ \bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r). \] Then, for each fixed $\delta\in(0,1/2)$ and every sufficiently large even $n$, the following conclusions hold. Here $k\geq0$ is the exponent of the logarithm in the angular assumption of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, taken from Proposition 3.1 of \citet{zhang2023wasserstein}; under that assumption the angular term at the threshold $t_n$ is of the order shown, up to this power of $\log n$. \begin{enumerate} \item If $s\in(0,1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}))$, take $t_n=\Theta(n^{(1-2s)\hat\gamma_n})$ and put $\kappa(v)=(1-2s)v$. With probability at least $1-\delta$, we have \[ \begin{aligned} |\widehat\theta_{n,t_n}^{\text{DR}}-\theta^{NETE}|=O\bigl(&\sqrt{V/\delta}\,n^{-s}+\log^2(6/\delta)n^{-1/2-s}\\ &+\bar e(t_n)+(\log n)^k n^{-s}+n^{-c_\alpha}\log n\log(6/\delta)+|\alpha|b_n\bigr). \end{aligned} \] \item If $s\in[1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}),1/2)$, take $t_n=\Theta(n^{\hat\gamma_n/(1+2\min\{1,\hat\gamma_n\})})$ and put $\kappa(v)=v/(1+2\min\{1,v\})$. Write $q=1/(2+\max\{\beta,1\})$. With probability at least $1-\delta$, we have \[ \begin{aligned} |\widehat\theta_{n,t_n}^{\text{DR}}-\theta^{NETE}|=O\bigl(&\sqrt{V/\delta}\,n^{-q}+\log^2(6/\delta)n^{-1/2-q}\\ &+\bar e(t_n)+(\log n)^k n^{-q}+n^{-c_\alpha}\log n\log(6/\delta)+|\alpha|b_n\bigr). \end{aligned} \] \end{enumerate}
- **Corollary A.4.** Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, further suppose that \begin{align*} R_p(n,\delta)&=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2}),\qquad R_g(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2}),\\ R_\alpha(n,\delta)&=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-c_\alpha}) \end{align*} for some $c_\alpha>0$. Suppose in addition that the conditions of Proposition~\ref{prop:id} hold. Let $\mathcal T$ consist of the training split and independent auxiliary data. Suppose that $\hat p$, $\hat g$ and $\hat\alpha_n$ are $\mathcal T$-measurable and are the same functions and exponent for every input threshold. Write $R_U=\|U\|$, $S_U=U/\|U\|$, $\gamma=1/\beta$, $\pi_t=\mathbb P(R_U>t)$, and \[ m_d^t(x,v)=\mathbb E[Y/R_U^\alpha\mid X=x,D=d,S_U=v,R_U>t]. \] In each case below, let $\kappa$ be the exponent function specified in that case. Use fixed constants $0<c_-\leq c_+<\infty$ in the threshold order, so that $c_-n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}\leq t_n\leq c_+n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}$, and define the deterministic band \[ \ell_n=(c_-/2)n^{\kappa(\gamma)},\qquad u_n=2c_+n^{\kappa(\gamma)},\qquad I_n=[\ell_n,u_n]. \] Suppose that with probability at least $1-\delta/6$, jointly for every $t\in I_n$, \[ \begin{aligned} |\hat\alpha_n-\alpha|&\leq R_\alpha(n,\delta/6),\\ |p(x)-\hat p(x)|&\leq R_p(n/2,\delta/6),\\ |m_d^t(x,v)-\hat g(x,d,v)|&\leq R_g(n_t,\delta/6), \end{aligned} \] simultaneously almost surely over the covariate and conditional direction support and both treatments, where $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$ is the training exceedance count. On this same event suppose that the fitted-exponent pseudo-outcome has finite conditional absolute mean for every $t\in I_n$ and that, for a constant $V<\infty$ independent of $n$ and $t$, \[ \mathbb E[\phi^2\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\leq V\qquad(t\in I_n),\qquad \phi=\frac{Y}{R_U^{\hat\alpha_n}}\left(\frac{D}{\hat p(X)}-\frac{1-D}{1-\hat p(X)}\right). \] Set the first-factor estimate to zero if there are no evaluation exceedances. Run the full-input Hill selection of Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} at tolerance $\delta/6$, and put \[ b_n=C_{\delta/6}\begin{cases} \left(\dfrac{\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&0<\beta<1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log n\,\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&\beta=1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/(2+\beta)},&\beta>1, \end{cases} \] with the constant supplied by Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}. Define \[ \bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r). \] Then, for each fixed $\delta\in(0,1/2)$ and every sufficiently large even $n$, the following conclusions hold. Here $k\geq0$ is the exponent of the logarithm in the angular assumption of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, taken from Proposition 3.1 of \citet{zhang2023wasserstein}; under that assumption the angular term at the threshold $t_n$ is of the order shown, up to this power of $\log n$. \begin{enumerate} \item If $s\in(0,1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}))$, take $t_n=\Theta(n^{(1-2s)\hat\gamma_n})$ and put $\kappa(v)=(1-2s)v$. With probability at least $1-\delta$, we have \[ \begin{aligned} |\widehat\theta_{n,t_n}^{\text{IPW}}-\theta^{NETE}|=O\bigl(&\sqrt{V/\delta}\,n^{-s}+\log(6/\delta)n^{-1/2}\\ &+\bar e(t_n)+(\log n)^k n^{-s}+n^{-c_\alpha}\log n\log(6/\delta)+|\alpha|b_n\bigr). \end{aligned} \] \item If $s\in[1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}),1/2)$, take $t_n=\Theta(n^{\hat\gamma_n/(1+2\min\{1,\hat\gamma_n\})})$ and put $\kappa(v)=v/(1+2\min\{1,v\})$. Write $q=1/(2+\max\{\beta,1\})$. With probability at least $1-\delta$, we have \[ \begin{aligned} |\widehat\theta_{n,t_n}^{\text{IPW}}-\theta^{NETE}|=O\bigl(&\sqrt{V/\delta}\,n^{-q}+\log(6/\delta)n^{-1/2}\\ &+\bar e(t_n)+(\log n)^k n^{-q}+n^{-c_\alpha}\log n\log(6/\delta)+|\alpha|b_n\bigr). \end{aligned} \] \end{enumerate}

## Clarifications (not errors)

Nothing in this section is an error of the paper. Each item is something the paper determines but does not say, made explicit so that the statements can be read, and formalized, in one way.

**How a statement is read.**

- **Proposition 3.3.** $U\perp(X,D)$.
- **Proposition 3.3.** $\|X\|_1\leqslant R$ almost surely.
- **Proposition 3.3.** For each norm, there exist $S$, $b_n\rightarrow\infty$, and $c>0$ such that, for every $a>0$, the polar measures $n\tmop{P}((\|U\|/b_n,U/\|U\|)\in\cdot)$ restricted to $[a,\infty]\times\mathbb{S}^+$ converge weakly as finite measures to the corresponding restriction of $c\nu_\beta\times S$.
- **Proposition 3.3.** $f$ is a fixed measurable version satisfying $f(X,D,U)=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X,D,U]$ almost surely, and this same version is used in Assumption~\ref{assump:f_homo}.
- **Proposition 3.3.** The homogeneity inequality holds for every $d\in\{0,1\}$ and every $t>0$, uniformly over $x\in B_R=\{x:\|x\|_1\leqslant R\}$ and $u\in S^{d-1}=\{u\in\mathbb R_+^d:\|u\|_1=1\}$, with $g$ being $L$-Lipschitz on this domain under the $\ell_1$ norm convention.
- **Proposition 3.3.** $Y(D)=Y$ almost surely throughout the paper.
- **Proposition 3.3.** The homogeneity inequality holds for every $d\in\{0,1\}$ and every radius $t>0$, uniformly over $x\in B_R$ and $u\in\mathbb R_+^d$ with $\|u\|_1=1$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** For each component law in $\tilde M_k$, there exists one constant $C_k>0$, independent of $z$, such that $h(z)=C_k\prod_{i=1}^{m}(1+z_i)^{-(\beta+1)}$ whenever $\|z\|_1>\zeta k^{(1-2s)/\beta}$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The existential cutoff $t_W$ is chosen sufficiently large for Lemmas~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} and~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw} and so that $\sup_{r\geq t_W}e(r)\leq1$ and $\mathbb P(\|U\|>tr)/\mathbb P(\|U\|>t)\leq4r^{-\beta}$ for every $t\geq t_W$ and $r\geq1$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** For each fixed $\mathcal T$-measurable choice $B>0$, the displayed probability bound uses the base conditions of Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}; its angular specialization additionally uses that lemma's angular assumption and $t\geq t_W$, and its variance alternative additionally requires $V_t^{\text{IPW}}<\infty$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** For each fixed $t$ and tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, the three nuisance inequalities, with $\delta=\varepsilon$, hold on one $\mathcal T$-measurable event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate support, the conditional direction support and both treatments, where $\mathcal T$ consists of the training split and independent auxiliary data.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The training indices are chosen uniformly from the subsets of $\{1,\ldots,n\}$ of size $n/2$, independently of the sampled observations; the remaining indices form $\mathcal D_2$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The equal split in Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} is defined for positive even sample sizes $n$, with $|\mathcal D_1|=|\mathcal D_2|=n/2$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The component dimension in the density of Assumption~\ref{assump:mvary} is $m=d_z$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** For each fixed $t$ and tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, the three nuisance inequalities, with $\delta=\varepsilon$, hold on one $\mathcal T$-measurable event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate support, the conditional direction support and both treatments.
- **Theorem 3.5.** Assumption~\ref{assump:mvary} specifies the noise law $\mathcal L(U)$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The fitted-exponent pseudo-outcome has finite conditional absolute mean under the evaluation tail law: $\mathbb E[|Y|/\|U\|^{\hat\alpha_n}\mid\mathcal D_1,\hbox{ auxiliary data},\|U\|>t]<\infty$ almost surely on each joint nuisance event.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The homogeneity inequality holds for both treatments and every positive radius, on the positive unit sphere for the $\ell_1$ norm.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The equal split uses $n\in2\mathbb N$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The symbol $V_t$ denotes $\tmop{Var}(\psi\mid\mathcal D_1,\hbox{ auxiliary data},\|U\|>t)$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The sample-size domain of $R_g(q,\varepsilon)$ includes every possible training exceedance count $q\in\{0,1,\ldots,n/2\}$, including $q=0$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1)$ there is a deterministic $n_0$ such that, for every integer $n\geq n_0$, the bound has probability at least $1-\delta$ under the law of the sample of size $n$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The count $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$ is the training-split exceedance count, with the training observations indexed first after the random split.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The sampling term comprises both $\sqrt{4v_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)/(\delta M)}$ and $(1+4/\delta)b_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)$.
- **Theorem 3.5.** The target $\eta$ denotes $\int\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]\,S(\tmop{ds})$, where $S$ is the limiting spectral probability measure.
- **Corollary 3.6.** For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, the probability guarantee quantifies separately over every sufficiently large even $n$.
- **Corollary 3.6.** The constants $c_-$ and $c_+$ in $t_n=\Theta(n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)})$ are deterministic and independent of $n$ and the observed sample.
- **Corollary A.4.** For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, the probability guarantee quantifies separately over every sufficiently large even $n$.
- **Corollary A.4.** The constants $c_-$ and $c_+$ in $t_n=\Theta(n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)})$ are deterministic and independent of $n$ and the observed sample.

**Notation.**

- $X$: $X\in\mathcal{X}$ denotes the covariate in the potential-outcome model. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $\|X\|_1\leqslant R$, so the covariate takes values in $B_R$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: In $\eta=\int\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]\,S(\tmop{ds})$, $\mathbb E_X$ integrates with respect to the population marginal law of $X$, rather than an empirical covariate distribution.
- $D$: $D\in\{0,1\}$ denotes the treatment indicator. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: In the proof's expansions $f(t)=C\beta t^{-\beta-1}(1+D(1-1/\beta)t^{-1}+o(t^{-1}))$ and $\bar F(t)=Ct^{-\beta}(1+Dt^{-1}+o(t^{-1}))$, $D$ denotes a fixed expansion coefficient rather than the treatment indicator.
- $Y$: $Y$ is the observed outcome satisfying consistency $Y=Y(D)$, where $Y(d)$ denotes the potential outcome when treatment is set to $d$.
- $Y(d)$: $Y(d)$ denotes the potential outcome under intervention $d\in\{0,1\}$, with consistency $Y(D)=Y$ throughout. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}: For both $d\in\{0,1\}$, $\mathbb E[Y(d)\mid X,U,D]=\mathbb E[Y(d)\mid X,U]$ almost surely, and $\mathbb E[|Y(d)|\mid\|U\|>t]<\infty$ for all sufficiently large $t$.
- $U$: $U$ denotes the extreme-noise vector in $\mathbb R_+^d$, with $\|U\|=\|U\|_1$ throughout the treatment-effect analysis. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: Assumption~\ref{assump:mvary} specifies the noise law $\mathcal L(U)$, with $U=AZ$ and $m=d_z$ in the component density. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: Assumption~\ref{assump:mvary} specifies the noise law $\mathcal L(U)$, with $U=AZ$ and $m=d_z$ in the component density.
- $d$: $d$ denotes the dimension in $U\in\mathbb R_+^d$ and in sphere notation; when used as the treatment argument in $Y(d)$, $f(x,d,u)$, or $g(x,d,u)$, it is a locally bound treatment value in $\{0,1\}$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $m\geq d\geq2$.
- $t$: $t>0$ is the level defining the strict exceedance event $\{\|U\|_1>t\}$, except when it is the argument of a scalar function in a tail calculation. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}: $t$ is a deterministic limit parameter tending to infinity. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The finite-threshold analysis uses a fixed deterministic $t$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The selected threshold is $t=t_n$, with $c_-n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}\leq t_n\leq c_+n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}$, where $0<c_-\leq c_+<\infty$ are deterministic and independent of $n$ and the observed sample. Here $\kappa(v)=(1-2s)v$ when $s\in(0,1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}))$, and $\kappa(v)=v/(1+2\min\{1,v\})$ when $s\in[1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}),1/2)$. The threshold-indexed nuisance and conditional-moment conditions hold jointly for every deterministic $t\in I_n=[(c_-/2)n^{\kappa(\gamma)},2c_+n^{\kappa(\gamma)}]$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: The bound concerns sufficiently large $t\geq1$, subject to $n\pi_t\geq16\log(4/\delta)$, where $\pi_t=\mathbb P(\|U\|>t)$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The bound concerns sufficiently large $t\geq1$, subject to $n\pi_t\geq16\log(4/\delta)$, where $\pi_t=\mathbb P(\|U\|>t)$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The main bounds concern deterministic $t\geq t_W$, subject to $n\pi_t\geq16\log(12/\delta)$, where $\pi_t=\mathbb P(\|U\|>t)$; the additional simplified bounds also require $t\geq\exp(1)$ and $a\log t\leq1$, where $a=R_\alpha(n,\delta/12)$.
- $\alpha$: $\alpha\in\mathbb R$ is the fixed population homogeneity exponent governing the normalization $f(x,d,tu)/t^\alpha$ and the definition of $\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}$; it is distinct from the supplied approximation $\hat\alpha_n$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $\alpha<\beta$.
- $\beta$: $\beta\in(0,\infty)$ is the population regular-variation index of the extreme-noise law, with radial limiting measure satisfying $\nu_\beta([r,\infty))=r^{-\beta}$ for $r>0$.
- $R$: $R$ is the fixed covariate bound in $\|X\|_1\leqslant R$, except where explicitly rebound as a local radial variable. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: In the proof, $R=\|U\|_1$ is the generic radial random variable; $f_R$, $F_R$, and $\bar F_R$ refer to its density, distribution function, and survival function. This local $R$ is distinct from the covariate bound.
- $f$: $f$ denotes a population function with its domain and meaning determined by its local definition, rather than an estimated regression function. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the extreme-effect model and its decompositions, $f$ is the real-valued population conditional-mean function satisfying $f(X,D,U)=\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X,D,U]$, whose pointwise values $f(x,d,u)$ are those appearing in the asymptotic homogeneous property. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: In the extreme-effect model and its decompositions, $f$ is the real-valued population conditional-mean function satisfying $f(X,D,U)=\mathbb E[Y\mid X,D,U]$, whose pointwise values $f(x,d,u)$ appear in the asymptotic homogeneous property.
- $g$: $g(x,d,u)$ is the population asymptotic normalized conditional-mean function, given by $g(x,d,u)=\lim_{t\to\infty}f(x,d,tu)/t^\alpha$ on its covariate, treatment, and direction domain. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: The homogeneity inequality $|f(x,d,tu)/t^\alpha-g(x,d,u)|\leqslant e(t)$ holds for every $d\in\{0,1\}$ and every $t>0$, uniformly over $x\in B_R$ and $u\in\mathbb R_+^d$ with $\|u\|_1=1$; $g$ is $L$-Lipschitz on this domain. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The homogeneity inequality $|f(x,d,tu)/t^\alpha-g(x,d,u)|\leqslant e(t)$ holds for every $d\in\{0,1\}$ and every $t>0$, uniformly over $x\in B_R$ and $u\in\mathbb R_+^d$ with $\|u\|_1=1$; $g$ is $L$-Lipschitz on this domain.
- $e$: $e(t)$ is a deterministic, nonnegative uniform approximation-error bound in the asymptotic homogeneous property, allowing $e(t)=0$ and tending to zero as $t\rightarrow\infty$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: The bound $|f(x,d,ru)/r^\alpha-g(x,d,u)|\leq e(r)$ holds for both $d\in\{0,1\}$ and every $r>0$, uniformly over $x\in B_R$ and $u\in\mathbb R_+^d$ with $\|u\|_1=1$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The bound $|f(x,d,ru)/r^\alpha-g(x,d,u)|\leq e(r)$ holds for both $d\in\{0,1\}$ and every $r>0$, uniformly over $x\in B_R$ and $u\in\mathbb R_+^d$ with $\|u\|_1=1$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The approximation-error envelope used in the bound is $\bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)$, and $G_t=C+\bar e(t)$; no equality $\bar e(t)=e(t)$ is assumed. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The approximation-error envelope used in the bound is $\bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)$, and $G_t=C+\bar e(t)$; no equality $\bar e(t)=e(t)$ is assumed. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The approximation-error envelope used in the bound is $\bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)$, evaluated at the selected threshold $t_n$; no equality $\bar e(t)=e(t)$ is assumed.
- $c$: $c$ denotes a deterministic positive constant whose identity and additional restrictions are supplied by its local definition. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the regular-variation definition, $c>0$ is the mass multiplier in $c\nu_\beta\times S$, independently of any overlap constant. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the overlap assumption, $c\in(0,1/2)$ bounds the estimated propensity uniformly by $c\leq\hat p(x)\leq1-c$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: In the regular-variation definition, $c>0$ is the mass multiplier in $c\nu_\beta\times S$, independently of the overlap constant; in the overlap assumption and the error bounds containing $1/c$, $c\in(0,1/2)$ satisfies $c\leq\hat p(x)\leq1-c$ for every $x\in\mathcal X$. The constants $c_1,c_2,c_\alpha$ are separate symbols.
- $p$: $p(x)=\mathbb P(D=1\mid X=x)$ is the population propensity function on $\mathcal X$, distinct from its fitted estimate $\hat p(x)$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $0<p(X)<1$ almost surely; this does not impose a uniform positive lower bound on $p$ or $1-p$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $0<p(X)<1$ almost surely; this does not impose a uniform positive lower bound on $p$ or $1-p$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: In the proof's rate derivation for $\beta\ne1$, $p=\min\{1,1/\beta\}$ is a fixed scalar exponent used in the choice of $k_0$ and the resulting convergence rate, rather than the propensity function. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: $0<p(X)<1$ almost surely; this does not impose a uniform positive lower bound on $p$ or $1-p$. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}: $0<p(X)<1$ almost surely for the population propensity; this does not impose a uniform positive lower bound on $p$ or $1-p$. In the proof's uniform-integrability argument when $\alpha>0$, the locally chosen $p>1$ with $p\alpha<\beta$ is a fixed scalar moment exponent in $|G(S_U)(R_U/t)^\alpha|^p$ and $(R_U/t)^{p\alpha}$.
- $\hat{p}$: $\hat p$ is the sample-dependent estimator of the propensity function fitted using the first half $\mathcal D_1$ of the split sample by regression of $D$ on $X$, with $\hat p(x)\in[c,1-c]$ for every $x\in\mathcal X$; the fitted function is fixed conditional on the training split and all randomness used in fitting. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: $\hat p$ is measurable with respect to $\mathcal D_1$ and independent auxiliary data; its error inequality holds jointly with the regression and exponent inequalities with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, uniformly almost surely over the covariate support, for each tolerance $\varepsilon$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $\hat p$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$, and is the same fitted function for every input threshold. With probability at least $1-\delta/6$, jointly for every $t\in I_n=[\ell_n,u_n]$, its inequality $|p(x)-\hat p(x)|\leq R_p(n/2,\delta/6)$ holds together with the regression and exponent inequalities, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate and conditional direction support and both treatments. The additional estimation-rate condition is $R_p(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2})$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: $\hat p$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$; for each fixed $t$ and $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, its inequality $|p(X)-\hat p(X)|\leq R_p(n,\varepsilon)$ holds jointly with the regression and exponent inequalities on one $\mathcal T$-measurable event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate support, the conditional direction support and both treatments. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $\hat p$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$; its error inequality holds jointly with the regression and exponent inequalities with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, uniformly almost surely over the covariate support, for each tolerance $\varepsilon$.
- $i$: $i$ is a locally bound integer index whose range is supplied by the expression in which it occurs. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: In $R_i=\|U_i\|_1$, $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$ indexes the full input sample; in the adaptive comparison, $i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}$ and the compared statistic is $H_n(i)=i^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{i}\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[i+1]})$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$, $i\in\{1,\ldots,n/2\}$ indexes the training observations, indexed first after the random split.
- $X_i$: $X_i\in\mathcal{X}$ is the covariate coordinate of the $i$th sampled tuple $(X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)$, indexed by $i=1,\ldots,N$ in the model and $i=1,\ldots,n$ in the estimation notation. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $X_i$ takes values in $B_R$, so $\|X_i\|_1\leq R$, under the bounded-covariate assumption. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: After the random equal split, $X_i$ is the covariate coordinate of a training observation for $1\leq i\leq n/2$ and of an evaluation observation for $n/2<i\leq n$.
- $D_i$: $D_i\in\{0,1\}$ is the observed-treatment coordinate of the $i$th sampled tuple $(X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)$, indexed by $i=1,\ldots,N$ in the model and $i=1,\ldots,n$ in the estimation notation. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: After the random equal split, $D_i$ is the treatment coordinate of a training observation for $1\leq i\leq n/2$ and of an evaluation observation for $n/2<i\leq n$.
- $Y_i$: $Y_i$ is the observed-outcome coordinate of the $i$th sampled tuple $(X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)$, indexed by $i=1,\ldots,N$ in the model and $i=1,\ldots,n$ in the estimation notation. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: After the random equal split, $Y_i$ is the observed-outcome coordinate of a training observation for $1\leq i\leq n/2$ and of an evaluation observation for $n/2<i\leq n$.
- $U_i$: $U_i$ denotes the extreme-noise vector of the observation with local sample index $i$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the training count $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$, $U_i$ belongs to the training split, whose observations are indexed first after the random split. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: $R_i=\|U_i\|_1$ for $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$ uses every full-input observation, without restriction to a split or exceedance set.
- $S$: As a spectral measure, $S$ is the population probability measure on $\mathbb{S}^+=\{u\in\mathbb{R}_+^d:\|u\|=1\}$ in the regular-variation limit, equivalently the limiting law of $U/\|U\|$ conditional on $\|U\|>t$ as $t\rightarrow\infty$, with the $\ell_1$ norm used after the notation convention.
- $b_n$: $b_n$ is a deterministic positive sequence on the index domain of its local definition. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the inherited regular-variation definition, $b_n$, $n\geq1$, is chosen for the law of $U$ and the chosen norm, tends to infinity, and satisfies $n\mathbb P((\|U\|/b_n,U/\|U\|)\in\cdot)\overset{w}{\rightarrow}c\nu_\beta\times S$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: In $Q_n$ and the radial estimation bound, $b_n=C_{\delta/6}\begin{cases}(\log((48/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/3},&0<\beta<1,\\(\log n\,\log((48/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/3},&\beta=1,\\(\log((48/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/(2+\beta)},&\beta>1.\end{cases}$ For fixed $\delta$ and the fixed noise law this is a deterministic error-bound sequence tending to zero, defined for sufficiently large $n$. The inherited regular-variation definition separately uses a normalizing sequence tending to infinity. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: In the radial estimation bound and the term $|\alpha|b_n$, $b_n=C_{\delta/6}\begin{cases}(\log((48/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/3},&0<\beta<1,\\(\log n\,\log((48/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/3},&\beta=1,\\(\log((48/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/(2+\beta)},&\beta>1.\end{cases}$ For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1/2)$ and fixed noise law, this is a deterministic error-bound sequence tending to zero, used for sufficiently large even $n$. The inherited regular-variation definition separately uses a normalizing sequence tending to infinity.
- $Z$: $Z$ denotes the random variable specified by its local definition: an illustrative regularly varying scalar, a nonnegative component vector in $U=AZ$, or a scalar truncated evaluation score. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $Z\in\mathbb{R}_+^{d_z}$ is the random component vector with $\mathcal{L}(Z)\in\tilde M_k$ for a positive integer $k$ in the population representation $U=AZ$; the approximation condition does not assert independence of its components. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: $Z\in\mathbb R_+^{d_z}$ is the random component vector with $\mathcal L(Z)\in\tilde M_k$ for a positive integer $k$ in the population representation $U=AZ$; the approximation condition does not assert independence of its components. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: In the tail-model assumption, $Z\in\mathbb R_+^{d_z}$ is the component vector with $\mathcal L(Z)\in\tilde M_k$ for a positive integer $k$, without an assertion of component independence. In the proof's conditional sampling argument, the local definition $Z=\psi I\{|\psi|\leq B\}$ denotes a scalar truncated evaluation score under the law conditional on $\mathcal T$ and $R_U>t$; $Z_i$ are its evaluation realizations. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the tail-model assumption, $Z\in\mathbb R_+^{d_z}$ is the component vector with $\mathcal L(Z)\in\tilde M_k$ for a positive integer $k$, without an assertion of component independence. In the proof's conditional sampling argument, the local definition $Z=\phi I\{|\phi|\leq B\}$ denotes a scalar truncated evaluation score under the law conditional on $\mathcal T$ and $R_U>t$; $Z_i$ are its evaluation realizations.
- $A$: In the representation $U=AZ$, $A$ is a nonnegative $d_u\times d_z$ matrix. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $A\in\mathcal{A}$ is a fixed matrix specifying the population representation $U=AZ$, rather than an estimated matrix or a matrix redrawn for each observation. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $A\in\mathcal A$ is a fixed matrix specifying the population representation $U=AZ$, rather than an estimated matrix or a matrix redrawn for each observation.
- $\tilde M_k$: $\tilde M_k$ is a fixed population class of component laws indexed by $k\in\{1,2,\ldots\}$, with fixed $\beta>0$, $0<s<1/2$, $0<\xi<1$, and $\zeta>0$; its joint-density conditions do not assert independence of the components. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $m=d_z$ in the defining density conditions, so the nominal density is $\beta^{d_z}\prod_{i=1}^{d_z}(1+z_i)^{-(\beta+1)}$ and every component product ranges over $i\in\{1,\ldots,d_z\}$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $m=d_z$ in the defining density conditions, so the nominal density is $\beta^{d_z}\prod_{i=1}^{d_z}(1+z_i)^{-(\beta+1)}$ and every component product ranges over $i\in\{1,\ldots,d_z\}$.
- $\mathcal{A}$: $\mathcal{A}$ is the fixed population matrix class, with column indices $i\in\{1,\ldots,d_z\}$ and fixed constants $0<l<1<u$ and $\sigma>0$.
- $M_k$: $M_k$ is a fixed population distribution class indexed by $k\in\{1,2,\ldots\}$, rather than by the sample size or a selected Hill order statistic. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $M_k=\{\mathcal L(U):U=AZ,\ A\in\mathcal A,\ \mathcal L(Z)\in\tilde M_k\}$; the class concerns the noise law, with $m=d_z$ in the component density.
- $M$: $M$ denotes the object specified by its local definition, either a class of probability laws or an observed exceedance count. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: $M=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}M_k$ is a class of noise laws, with $M_k=\{\mathcal L(U):U=AZ,\ A\in\mathcal A,\ \mathcal L(Z)\in\tilde M_k\}$ and $m=d_z$ in the component density. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: In the score bound and the zero-count convention, $M=|\mathcal I|$ is the realized second-half evaluation exceedance count. The tail-model assumption separately concerns the noise law $\mathcal L(U)$, with $m=d_z$ in its component density. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: In the sampling terms, count bound and zero-count convention, $M=|\mathcal I|$ is the realized second-half evaluation exceedance count. In the inherited tail-model definition, $M=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}M_k$ separately denotes the class of noise laws, with component dimension $m=d_z$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the score bound and zero-count convention, $M=|\mathcal I|$ is the realized second-half evaluation exceedance count. The tail-model assumption separately concerns the noise law $\mathcal L(U)$, with $m=d_z$ in its component density.
- $s$: In the tail model, $s\in(0,1/2)$ is a fixed model constant governing both the density error $\xi k^{-s}$ and the exact-tail threshold $\zeta k^{(1-2s)/\beta}$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: In $m_d^t(x,s)$, $S_U=s$, $s\mapsto\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]$, and $\int\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]\,S(\tmop{ds})$, $s\in\mathbb S^+=\{u\in\mathbb R_+^d:\|u\|_1=1\}$ is an angular argument or bound integration variable. In $t^{-\beta s/(1-2s)}$, $s$ retains its scalar tail-model meaning. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $s$ is the scalar tail-model constant in the threshold exponent and the two item restrictions: the first item assumes $0<s<1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\})$, and the second assumes $1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\})\leqslant s<1/2$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In the proof's $m_d^t(x,s)$ and $s\mapsto\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]$, $s\in\mathbb S^+=\{u\in\mathbb R_+^d:\|u\|_1=1\}$ is an angular argument, distinct from the scalar tail-model constant. In Proposition~\ref{prop:id}: In the proof's $G(s)=\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]$, $h_t(r,s)=G(s)r^\alpha$, and $\int G(s)\,S(\tmop{ds})$, $s\in\mathbb S^+=\{u\in\mathbb R_+^d:\|u\|_1=1\}$ is an angular argument or bound integration variable.
- $\sigma$: $\sigma>0$ is a fixed model constant imposing $JA=\sqrt{\det(A^\mathsf{T}A)}\geq\sigma$ on matrices in $\mathcal{A}$.
- $\xi$: $\xi\in(0,1)$ is a fixed model constant bounding the relative density approximation error by $\xi k^{-s}$.
- $\zeta$: $\zeta>0$ is a fixed constant in the tail-model class: for each class index $k$, the exact proportional-density region is $\|z\|_1>\zeta k^{(1-2s)/\beta}$.
- $n$: $n$ is the size of the full input dataset $\mathcal{D}=\{(X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)\}_{i=1}^n$, before its split into two equal halves; when used as the argument of an estimation-error function, it denotes that function's sample-size argument. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: $n$ is the integer size of the full input sample whose radii are ordered, with $n\geq2$ for the definition of $H_n(k)$; for each fixed $\delta\in(0,1)$ there is a deterministic $n_0$ such that the deviation bound holds with probability at least $1-\delta$ under the sample-size-$n$ law for every integer $n\geq n_0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The full-input sample size $n$ is a positive even integer, with $|\mathcal D_1|=|\mathcal D_2|=n/2$; this restriction does not restrict a generic sample-size argument of an estimation-error function to even integers. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, the probability guarantee holds separately under the sample-size-$n$ law for every sufficiently large even $n$.
- $\delta$: $\delta$ is a fixed confidence tolerance in $(0,1)$, used in probability guarantees of the form $1-\delta$ and in the adaptive Hill tuning rule. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1)$ there is a deterministic $n_0$ such that, for every integer $n\geq n_0$, the bound has probability at least $1-\delta$ under the law of the sample of size $n$; $n_0$ and $C_\delta$ may depend on $\delta$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: $\delta\in(0,1/2)$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1/2)$, the probability guarantee holds separately under the sample-size-$n$ law for every sufficiently large even $n$; the sample-size requirement may depend on $\delta$. The full-input Hill selection uses tolerance $\delta/6$.
- $n_t$: $n_t$ is the observed number of threshold exceedances in the designated half of the sample, with the half and strictness of the threshold determined by its local definition. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$ is the realized training-split exceedance count for positive even $n$, taking values in $\{0,\ldots,n/2\}$; it is distinct from the evaluation count $|\mathcal I|$ and from its expectation $n\mathbb P(\|U\|>t)/2$.
- $R_p$: $R_p(n,\delta)$ is a deterministic positive error-bound function, monotonically decreasing in its sample-size argument, used in the high-probability inequality $|p(X)-\hat p(X)|\leqslant R_p(n,\delta)$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $R_p(n,\varepsilon)=\Theta(\log(1/\varepsilon)n^{-1/2})$. On one joint nuisance event of probability at least $1-\delta/6$, the three nuisance inequalities hold simultaneously for every $t\in I_n$, almost surely over the covariate and conditional direction support and both treatments; the propensity inequality is $|p(x)-\hat p(x)|\leqslant R_p(n/2,\delta/6)$, using the same fitted propensity for every input threshold. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: For each fixed $t$ and tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, the three nuisance inequalities hold on one $\mathcal T$-measurable event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate support, conditional direction support and both treatments. The displayed propensity inequality uses $R_p(n,\varepsilon)$; the training-split propensity bound entering the final error bounds uses $R_p(n/2,\varepsilon)$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: For each tolerance $\varepsilon$, the nuisance inequalities hold jointly with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, uniformly almost surely over the covariate and direction support and both treatments. The training-split propensity bound used in the proof is $|p(x)-\hat p(x)|\leqslant R_p(n/2,\varepsilon)$.
- $R_g$: $R_g(q,\delta)$ is a nonnegative regression-error bound function, nonincreasing in its sample-size argument $q$, for estimating the conditional mean of $Y/\|U\|^\alpha$ given $(X,D,U/\|U\|)$ and $\|U\|>t$; evaluation at $q=n_t$ uses the observed tail count. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $R_g(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2})$. On one joint nuisance event of probability at least $1-\delta/6$, simultaneously for every $t\in I_n$, $|m_d^t(x,v)-\hat g(x,d,v)|\leqslant R_g(n_t,\delta/6)$ almost surely over the covariate and conditional direction support and both treatments, where $m_d^t(x,v)=\mathbb E[Y/\|U\|^\alpha\mid X=x,D=d,U/\|U\|=v,\|U\|>t]$ and $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$ is the training-split exceedance count. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: For each tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, the bound $R_g(n_t,\varepsilon)$ applies uniformly almost surely over the covariate and direction support and both treatments, on the joint nuisance event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$; its sample-size argument $n_t$ is the training-split exceedance count, distinct from $M=|\mathcal I|$. For positive even $n$, its domain includes every $q\in\{0,1,\ldots,n/2\}$, including $q=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: For each fixed $t$ and tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, the regression-error bound $R_g(n_t,\varepsilon)$ holds together with the propensity and exponent inequalities on one $\mathcal T$-measurable event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate support, conditional direction support, and both treatments; $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$ is the training-split exceedance count, with training observations indexed first after the random split. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: For each tolerance $\varepsilon$, $R_g(n_t,\varepsilon)$ applies uniformly almost surely over the covariate and direction support and both treatments on the joint nuisance event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$; $n_t$ is the training-split exceedance count, distinct from $M=|\mathcal I|$. Its sample-size domain includes every possible training exceedance count $q\in\{0,1,\ldots,n/2\}$, including $q=0$.
- $R_\alpha$: $R_\alpha(n,\delta)$ is a deterministic positive error-bound function, monotonically decreasing in its sample-size argument, used in the high-probability inequality $|\hat\alpha_n-\alpha|\leqslant R_\alpha(n,\delta)$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-c_\alpha})$ for some $c_\alpha>0$.
- $\widehat{\theta}_{n,t}^{\text{DR}}$: $\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\text{DR}}$ is the sample estimator formed by multiplying the split-sample normalized DR first-factor estimate by $\hat\mu_n=(1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n)^{-1}$, wherever that denominator and the first-factor estimate are defined. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}: The estimator is evaluated at $t_n=\Theta(n^{(1-2s)\hat\gamma_n})$ in the first item and $t_n=\Theta(n^{\hat\gamma_n/(1+2\min\{1,\hat\gamma_n\})})$ in the second item, with deterministic positive order constants independent of $n$ and the sample. The fitted $\hat p$, $\hat g$, and $\hat\alpha_n$ are measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$ and are the same functions and exponent for every input threshold. The Hill estimate $\hat\gamma_n$ uses all $n$ input radii at tolerance $\delta/6$. Use $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}=0$ when there are no evaluation exceedances and $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The threshold $t$ is deterministic, the fitted propensity, regression, and exponent are measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$, and $\hat\gamma_n=\widetilde\gamma_n$ is computed from all $n$ input radii at tolerance $\delta/6$; use $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}=0$ when $M=0$ and $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n=0$.
- $\widehat{\theta}_{n,t}^{\text{IPW}}$: $\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\text{IPW}}$ is the split-sample product $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}\hat\mu_n$, using $\hat p$ fitted on $\mathcal D_1$, the supplied exponent $\hat\alpha_n$, and the normalized IPW score averaged over evaluation exceedances in $\mathcal D_2$, with $\hat\mu_n=(1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n)^{-1}$ wherever the average and reciprocal are defined. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The estimator is evaluated at $t_n=\Theta(n^{(1-2s)\hat\gamma_n})$ in the first item and $t_n=\Theta(n^{\hat\gamma_n/(1+2\min\{1,\hat\gamma_n\})})$ in the second item, with deterministic positive order constants independent of $n$ and the sample. The fitted $\hat p$, $\hat g$, and $\hat\alpha_n$ are measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$ and are the same functions and exponent for every input threshold. The Hill estimate $\hat\gamma_n$ uses all $n$ input radii at tolerance $\delta/6$. Use $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}=0$ when there are no evaluation exceedances and $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The threshold $t$ is deterministic, the fitted propensity, regression, and exponent are measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$, and $\hat\gamma_n=\widetilde\gamma_n$ is computed from all $n$ input radii at tolerance $\delta/6$; use $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}=0$ when $M=0$ and $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n=0$.
- $\hat{\eta}_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}$: $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}$ is the average normalized doubly robust score over exceedances in the second half $\mathcal{D}_2$, using $\hat p$ and $\hat g$ fitted on the first half $\mathcal{D}_1$ and the supplied exponent $\hat\alpha_n$, wherever $|\mathcal{I}|>0$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: Set $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}=0$ when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$; conditional on $\mathcal D_1$ and the independent auxiliary data, the fitted $\hat p$, $\hat g$, and $\hat\alpha_n$ are fixed, and for $M>0$ the estimator averages the displayed score $\psi$ over the evaluation exceedances. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}: The estimator is evaluated at $t_n=\Theta(n^{(1-2s)\hat\gamma_n})$ in the first item and $t_n=\Theta(n^{\hat\gamma_n/(1+2\min\{1,\hat\gamma_n\})})$ in the second item, with deterministic positive order constants independent of $n$ and the sample. Set $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}=0$ when there are no evaluation exceedances. The fitted $\hat p$, $\hat g$, and $\hat\alpha_n$ are $\mathcal T$-measurable, where $\mathcal T$ consists of the training split and independent auxiliary data, and are the same functions and exponent for every input threshold; they are held fixed in the score moments conditional on $\mathcal T$ and $\|U\|>t$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: Set $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}=0$ when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$; conditional on the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$, the fitted $\hat p$, $\hat g$, and $\hat\alpha_n$ are fixed, and for $M>0$ the estimator averages $\psi^{\mathrm{DR}}$ over the evaluation exceedances. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: Set $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}=0$ when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$; conditional on the training split and independent auxiliary data $\mathcal T$, the fitted $\hat p$, $\hat g$, and $\hat\alpha_n$ are fixed, and for $M>0$ the estimator averages the normalized doubly robust score over the evaluation exceedances.
- $\hat{\eta}_{n,t}^{\text{IPW}}$: $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}$ is the sample average over the threshold exceedances in the second half $\mathcal{D}_2$ of the split sample, using $\hat p$ fitted on the first half $\mathcal{D}_1$ and the supplied exponent $\hat\alpha_n$, wherever $|\mathcal{I}|>0$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The estimator is evaluated at $t_n=\Theta(n^{(1-2s)\hat\gamma_n})$ in the first item and $t_n=\Theta(n^{\hat\gamma_n/(1+2\min\{1,\hat\gamma_n\})})$ in the second item, with deterministic positive order constants independent of $n$ and the sample. Set $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}=0$ when there are no evaluation exceedances. The fitted propensity and exponent are $\mathcal T$-measurable, where $\mathcal T$ consists of the training split and independent auxiliary data, and are the same function and exponent for every input threshold; they are held fixed in the score moments conditional on $\mathcal T$ and $\|U\|>t$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}=0$ when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$. The fitted propensity and exponent are measurable with respect to $\mathcal T$, the training split together with independent auxiliary data, and are held fixed in the score moments conditional on $\mathcal T$ and $\|U\|>t$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}=0$ when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$. The fitted propensity and exponent are measurable with respect to $\mathcal T$, the training split together with independent auxiliary data, and are held fixed in the score moments conditional on $\mathcal T$ and $\|U\|>t$; the threshold $t$ is deterministic.
- $\eta$: $\eta$ is a population quantity whose scalar or functional meaning is determined by its local use. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: $\eta=\int\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]\,S(\mathrm{d}s)$, where $S$ is the limiting spectral probability measure. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: $\eta=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathbb E[g(X,1,U/\|U\|)-g(X,0,U/\|U\|)\mid\|U\|>t]$ is the scalar population first-factor target. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $\eta=\int\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]\,S(\tmop{ds})$, where $S$ is the limiting spectral probability measure. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: $\eta(v)=vQ'(v)/Q(v)-\gamma=\bar F_R(Q(v))/(Q(v)f_R(Q(v)))-1/\beta$ is the deterministic von Mises function on the differentiable tail of $Q(v)=F_R^{-1}(1-1/v)$, where $R=\|U\|_1$, $F_R$ is its population distribution function, $\bar F_R$ its survival function, $f_R$ its density, and $\gamma=1/\beta$.
- $\mathcal{D}$: $\mathcal D=\{(X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)\}_{i=1}^n$ denotes the indexed input dataset of $n$ observations. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The sample size $n$ is a positive even integer, and the equal split satisfies $|\mathcal D_1|=|\mathcal D_2|=n/2$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The input size $n$ is a positive even integer; after the random equal split, indices $1,\ldots,n/2$ designate the training observations and indices $n/2+1,\ldots,n$ designate the evaluation observations.
- $\mathcal{D}_1$: $\mathcal D_1$ is the first half of the random equal split of the indexed dataset $\mathcal D$, containing $n/2$ observations and supplying the observations for nuisance regression. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The sample size $n$ is a positive even integer, and $|\mathcal D_1|=|\mathcal D_2|=n/2$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The input size $n$ is a positive even integer, and after the random split the training observations are indexed first, so $\mathcal D_1=\{(X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)\}_{i=1}^{n/2}$ and $n_t=\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}I(\|U_i\|>t)$ counts its exceedances.
- $\mathcal{D}_2$: $\mathcal D_2$ is the second equal part of the split dataset, used for evaluation, with the equal split defined for positive even sample sizes $n$ and $|\mathcal D_1|=|\mathcal D_2|=n/2$.
- $\hat{\alpha}_n$: $\hat{\alpha}_n\in\mathbb{R}$ is the supplied approximate value of the outcome scaling index $\alpha$, obtainable from prior knowledge or heuristics. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: $\hat{\alpha}_n$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data; for each tolerance $\varepsilon$, its bound $|\hat{\alpha}_n-\alpha|\leq R_\alpha(n,\varepsilon)$ holds jointly with the other nuisance inequalities with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$. The bound uses $a=R_\alpha(n,\delta/4)\leq a_0$ for fixed $a_0\in(0,\beta)$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: $\hat\alpha_n$ is measurable with respect to $\mathcal T$, the training split and independent auxiliary data. For every fixed $t$ and tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, $|\hat\alpha_n-\alpha|\leq R_\alpha(n,\varepsilon)$ holds on one $\mathcal T$-measurable event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, jointly with the other nuisance inequalities, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate support, conditional direction support and both treatments. The bound uses $a=R_\alpha(n,\delta/12)\leq a_0$ for fixed $a_0\in(0,\beta)$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $\hat{\alpha}_n$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data; for every tolerance $\varepsilon$, its inequality $|\hat{\alpha}_n-\alpha|\leq R_\alpha(n,\varepsilon)$ holds jointly with the other nuisance inequalities with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$. The exponent-error quantity is $a=R_\alpha(n,\delta/4)\leq a_0$, where $a_0\in(0,\beta)$ is fixed. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $\hat\alpha_n$ is $\mathcal T$-measurable, where $\mathcal T$ consists of the training split and independent auxiliary data, and is the same exponent for every input threshold. Its rate satisfies $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-c_\alpha})$ for some fixed $c_\alpha>0$. With probability at least $1-\delta/6$, the inequality $|\hat\alpha_n-\alpha|\leq R_\alpha(n,\delta/6)$ holds jointly with the propensity and regression inequalities for every $t\in I_n=[(c_-/2)n^{\kappa(\gamma)},2c_+n^{\kappa(\gamma)}]$, simultaneously almost surely over the covariate and conditional direction support and both treatments.
- $\hat{g}$: $\hat g(x,d,s)$ is the fitted regression of $Y/\|U\|^{\hat\alpha_n}$ on $(X,D,U/\|U\|)$ using $\mathcal D_1$, with $s\in\mathbb S^+$ for the paper's $\ell_1$ norm. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: $\hat g$ is measurable with respect to $\mathcal D_1$ and independent auxiliary data and is held fixed in the conditional evaluation-score expectations; its nuisance inequality holds uniformly almost surely over the covariate and direction support and both treatments on the stipulated joint nuisance events. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: For each fixed $t$ and tolerance $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, $\hat g$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data, denoted together by $\mathcal T$, and is held fixed in the conditional evaluation-score expectations; its nuisance comparison is with $\mathbb E[Y/\|U\|^{\alpha}\mid X,D,U/\|U\|,\|U\|>t]$, with bound $R_g(n_t,\varepsilon)$, although the fitted response uses $\hat\alpha_n$. This inequality holds simultaneously almost surely over the covariate support, conditional direction support and both treatments on one $\mathcal T$-measurable joint nuisance event of probability at least $1-\varepsilon$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $\hat g$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data, denoted together by $\mathcal T$; its nuisance inequality holds uniformly almost surely over the covariate and direction support and both treatments on the stipulated joint nuisance events. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $\hat g$ is measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data, denoted together by $\mathcal T$, and is the same fitted function for every input threshold; with probability at least $1-\delta/6$, jointly for every $t\in I_n$, $|m_d^t(x,v)-\hat g(x,d,v)|\leq R_g(n_t,\delta/6)$ simultaneously almost surely over the covariate and conditional direction support and both treatments, where $m_d^t(x,v)=\mathbb E[Y/\|U\|^\alpha\mid X=x,D=d,U/\|U\|=v,\|U\|>t]$ and $n_t$ is the training exceedance count.
- $\mathcal{I}$: $\mathcal I=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}: (X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)\in\mathcal D_2,\ \|U_i\|>t\}$ is the evaluation exceedance index set at the threshold supplied to the estimator. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: At the selected threshold $t_n$, $\mathcal I=\{i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}:(X_i,D_i,Y_i,U_i)\in\mathcal D_2,\ \|U_i\|>t_n\}$.
- $\hat{\gamma}_n$: $\hat\gamma_n$ denotes a sample EVI estimate computed from ordered $\ell_1$ radii; the displayed definition in Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} is $\hat\gamma_n=k^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^k\log(\|U_{(j)}\|/\|U_{(j+1)}\|)$ on the evaluation exceedances, with the displayed adaptive choice of $k$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The full-input convention is $\hat\gamma_n=\widetilde\gamma_n=H_n(\widetilde k_n)$ at tolerance $\delta/6$. Here $R_{[1]}\geq\cdots\geq R_{[n]}$ order all input radii, $H_n(k)=k^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^k\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[k+1]})$ for $1\leq k\leq n-1$, and $\widetilde k_n$ is the maximum $k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}$ satisfying $|H_n(i)-H_n(k)|\leq H_n(i)\rho_n(\delta/6)/\sqrt i$ for every $i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}$, using the displayed tuning definitions for $L_n$ and $\rho_n$.
- $t_n$: $t_n$ denotes the selected threshold sequence, depending on $\hat\gamma_n$, with $t_n=\Theta(n^{(1-2s)\hat\gamma_n})$ for $s\in(0,1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}))$ and $t_n=\Theta(n^{\hat\gamma_n/(1+2\min\{1,\hat\gamma_n\})})$ for $s\in[1/(2+\max\{1,\beta\}),1/2)$; the $t$ in the corresponding estimator denotes this selected threshold. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The threshold order means $c_-n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}\leq t_n\leq c_+n^{\kappa(\hat\gamma_n)}$, with deterministic constants $0<c_-\leq c_+<\infty$ independent of $n$ and the observed sample; $\kappa(v)=(1-2s)v$ in the first regime and $\kappa(v)=v/(1+2\min\{1,v\})$ in the second. The estimate $\hat\gamma_n$ is obtained by full-input Hill selection at tolerance $\delta/6$.
- $S_i$: $S_i=U_i/\|U_i\|_1$ for $i\in\mathcal{I}$ is the observed direction at the finite threshold $t$, taking values in $\mathbb{S}^+$.
- $l_n$: $l_n$ is the deterministic integer lower endpoint of the adaptive Hill candidate range, with $1\leq l_n\leq n$ wherever that range is used. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The algorithm's lower candidate index is $l_n=\min\{n-1,\lceil100\log n\rceil\}=L_n$ for $n\geq2$, and its candidate range is $\{l_n,\ldots,n-1\}$.
- $k$: $k$ is context-dependent: in adaptive Hill estimation it is a locally bound integer candidate or the data-dependent selected integer, in $M=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\infty}M_k$ it is a locally bound positive integer model-class index, and in an angular logarithmic factor it is a fixed nonnegative real exponent distinct from both indices. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: $k$ in $H_n(k)$ is a candidate integer with $1\leq k\leq n-1$. The selected index is $\widetilde k_n=\max\{k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}:|H_n(i)-H_n(k)|\leq H_n(i)\rho_n(\delta)/\sqrt i\ \text{for every }i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}\}$, where $L_n=\min\{n-1,\lceil100\log n\rceil\}$; $H_n$ uses all $n$ input radii. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The selected radial index is $\widetilde k_n=\max\{k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}:|H_n(i)-H_n(k)|\leq H_n(i)\rho_n(\delta/6)/\sqrt i\ \text{for every }i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}\}$, where $L_n=\min\{n-1,\lceil100\log n\rceil\}$ and $H_n$ uses all $n$ input radii. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The radial step uses all $n$ input radii and selects $k=\max\{q\in\{l_n,\ldots,n-1\}:|\hat\gamma(i)-\hat\gamma(q)|\leq\hat\gamma(i)r_n(\delta)/\sqrt i\ \text{for every }i\in\{l_n,\ldots,q\}\}$, with $l_n=\min\{n-1,\lceil100\log n\rceil\}$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The selected radial index is $\widetilde k_n=\max\{k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}:|H_n(i)-H_n(k)|\leq H_n(i)\rho_n(\delta/6)/\sqrt i\ \text{for every }i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}\}$, where $L_n=\min\{n-1,\lceil100\log n\rceil\}$ and $H_n$ uses all $n$ input radii. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: In the angular bound $W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\|U\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\|U\|})\leq C_W(\log t)^k t^{-\min\{1,\beta\}}+C_Wt^{-\beta s/(1-2s)}$, $k\geq0$ is a fixed exponent supplied by the angular assumption, independent of the Hill selection; the same exponent appears in the corresponding factor $(\log n)^k$.
- $r_n$: $r_n$ is a deterministic positive tolerance function of the confidence argument $\delta$, indexed by the integer sample size $n$, rather than an estimated quantity. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: For a confidence argument $\varepsilon\in(0,1)$, the algorithm uses $J_n=1+\lceil\log n/\log2\rceil$, $q_n(\varepsilon)=\log(4J_n/\varepsilon)$ and $r_n(\varepsilon)=16\{2\sqrt{q_n(\varepsilon)}+q_n(\varepsilon)/\sqrt{l_n}\}=\rho_n(\varepsilon)$, where $l_n=L_n=\min\{n-1,\lceil100\log n\rceil\}$.
- $\hat{\gamma}_i$: $\hat\gamma_i$ is the common-denominator Hill statistic computed from all $n$ input radii ordered as $\|U_{(1)}\|\geq\cdots\geq\|U_{(n)}\|$, for integers $1\leq i\leq n-1$ with positive reference radius $\|U_{(i+1)}\|$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The candidate indexed Hill statistics in the radial selection are $H_n(i)=i^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^i\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[i+1]})$, computed from all $n$ input radii, with $1\leq i\leq n-1$; the selection compares candidates $i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}$ for $k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}$ at tolerance $\delta/6$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The candidate indexed Hill statistics are $H_n(i)=i^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^i\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[i+1]})$, computed from all $n$ input radii, with $1\leq i\leq n-1$; the selection compares candidates $i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}$ for $k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}$ at tolerance $\delta/6$.
- $\hat{\mu}_n$: $\hat{\mu}_n=(1-\hat{\alpha}_n\hat{\gamma}_n)^{-1}$ is the sample plug-in estimate of the limiting radial moment $(1-\alpha\gamma)^{-1}$, defined when $1-\hat{\alpha}_n\hat{\gamma}_n\ne0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n=0$, and $\hat\mu_n=(1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n)^{-1}$ otherwise.
- $j$: In radial order-statistic notation, $j$ is a locally bound integer rank or summation index, ranging over the ranks specified by its display. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}: In $H_n(k)=k^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^k\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[k+1]})$, $j$ ranges over $\{1,\ldots,k\}$, with $1\leq k\leq n-1$; the denominator is the common full-input radius $R_{[k+1]}$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: In the full-input Hill output, $j$ ranges over $\{1,\ldots,\widetilde k_n\}$ in $H_n(\widetilde k_n)=\widetilde k_n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde k_n}\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[\widetilde k_n+1]})$; the denominator is common to every summand. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: In $\hat\gamma(k)=k^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^k\log(\|U_{(j)}\|/\|U_{(k+1)}\|)$, $j\in\{1,\ldots,k\}$ with $1\leq k\leq n-1$, and the denominator is common to every summand. In the equivalent expression $k^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^k j\log(\|U_{(j)}\|/\|U_{(j+1)}\|)$, each adjacent spacing has weight $j$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: In the full-input Hill output, $j$ ranges over $\{1,\ldots,\widetilde k_n\}$ in $H_n(\widetilde k_n)=\widetilde k_n^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde k_n}\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[\widetilde k_n+1]})$; the denominator is common to every summand.
- $U_{(j)}$: $U_{(j)}$ denotes a noise observation ranked by decreasing $\ell_1$ norm within the collection used by the radial step; only its norm is used, and it is defined only for ranks present in that collection. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The radial output uses all $n$ input radii $R_i=\|U_i\|_1$, ordered as $R_{[1]}\geq\cdots\geq R_{[n]}$, and is $\hat\gamma_n=\widetilde\gamma_n$. Thus the radial ranks refer to the full input rather than to $\{U_i:i\in\mathcal I\}$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: $U_{(j)}$ is ranked within all $n$ input noise observations, with $\|U_{(1)}\|\geq\cdots\geq\|U_{(n)}\|$; it is defined for $j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$. The radial step uses this full-input collection. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The radial output uses all $n$ input radii $R_i=\|U_i\|_1$, ordered as $R_{[1]}\geq\cdots\geq R_{[n]}$, with the full-input Hill selection run at tolerance $\delta/6$. Thus the radial ranks refer to the full input rather than to $\{U_i:i\in\mathcal I\}$.
- $\widehat{\theta}_{n,t}^{\mathrm{estimator}}$: $\widehat{\theta}_{n,t}^{\mathrm{estimator}}$ denotes the sample estimator computed from the supplied inputs and split dataset, with nuisance functions fitted on $\mathcal D_1$, the score average computed using the exceedances in $\mathcal D_2$, and the radial factor computed using all $n$ input radii, with $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n=0$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}: The score factor $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}$ is set to zero when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$. In Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}: The score factor $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}$ is set to zero when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$. In Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}: The radial estimate uses $\hat\gamma_n=\widetilde\gamma_n$ computed from all input radii by the adaptive Hill selection at tolerance $\delta/6$; each score factor is set to zero when $M=|\mathcal I|=0$, and $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $|1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n|=0$. In Corollary~\ref{cor:rate}, Corollary~\ref{cor:rate_ipw}: The estimator is evaluated at the selected threshold $t_n$, using the same $\mathcal T$-measurable nuisance functions $\hat p$, $\hat g$ and exponent $\hat\alpha_n$ for every input threshold; its radial estimate uses the full-input Hill selection at tolerance $\delta/6$, and its first-factor estimate is zero when there are no evaluation exceedances.

**Objections raised during the review and withdrawn.** The paper's owner showed that each misreads the paper; the printed statement stands under the reading given.

- **Corollary 3.6.** In the rate assumptions $R_p(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2})$, $R_g(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2})$ and $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-c_\alpha})$, the dependence on the tolerance $\delta$ is the displayed factor $\log(1/\delta)$: the implied constants and the sample size from which the bounds hold do not depend on $\delta$.
- **Corollary A.4.** In the rate assumptions $R_p(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2})$, $R_g(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/2})$ and $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=\Theta(\log(1/\delta)n^{-c_\alpha})$, the dependence on the tolerance $\delta$ is the displayed factor $\log(1/\delta)$: the implied constants and the sample size from which the bounds hold do not depend on $\delta$.
- **a condition or definition of the paper.** In Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} the outputs are given a value on every sample by convention: when the index set $\mathcal I$ is empty the averages defining $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}$ and $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}$ are taken to be $0$, and a Hill statistic whose reference radius $\|U_{(k+1)}\|$ is $0$ is taken to be $0$. These conventions concern events the results exclude: the bounds are stated on events where $|\mathcal I|\geq1$, and the radii are positive almost surely.
- **a condition or definition of the paper.** In Assumption~\ref{assump:overlap} the overlap constant $c\in(0,1/2)$ is one deterministic constant: it does not depend on the sample size $n$, on the sample, or on the threshold $t$.

| findings | [refuted, V1.5] The core carries the printed hypotheses and identification setting faithfully. The homogeneity display could quantify over one fixed treatment argument and radius, or uniformly over both treatment arguments and all positive radii; its sphere could also mean the usual Euclidean sphere or the positive unit sphere for the paper's norm. The proof and global norm convention determine the latter, uniform reading. The substantive error is the proof's first equality: $(Y(1),Y(0))\perp D\mid X$ and $U\perp(X,D)$ do not imply conditional exogeneity given $(X,U)$. The conclusion fails even with overlap, exact homogeneity, finite outcome means, and $\alpha<\beta$. In that moment range, the remaining radial-angular passage can be closed using Potter bounds, a higher radial moment for uniform integrability, and $\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)\to0$; bounded first moments alone do not establish uniform integrability. The preliminary radial-moment sentence also drops the additive one, although the subsequent calculation supplies the correct value $\beta/(\beta-\alpha)$. witness: Take independent $X\sim\text{Unif}([0,1])$, $D\sim\text{Ber}(1/2)$, $V\sim\text{Unif}([0,1])$, and $W$ with $\mathbb P(W>w)=w^{-3}$ for $w\geq1$. Set $U=W(V,1-V)$, $\alpha=1$, and $T(w)=(1-w^{-3})^{-1/3}$. Define $Y(1)=DW+(1-D)T(W)$, $Y(0)=0$, and $Y=Y(D)$. Since $W^{-3}$ is uniform, $T(W)$ has the same law as $W$. Thus $(Y(1),Y(0))\perp D\mid X$, and $U\perp(X,D)$; independent copies satisfy the sampling assumption. Nevertheless, $f(x,1,u)=\\|u\\|$, $f(x,0,u)=0$, so exact homogeneity holds with $g(x,1,s)=1$, $g(x,0,s)=0$, and $e(t)=0$. The printed right-hand side is $3/2$. But $\mathbb E[W\mid W>t]=3t/2$ and $\mathbb E[T(W)\mid W>t]\to1$, giving $\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}=3/4$. The false step replaces the conditional counterfactual mean by the observed conditional mean after conditioning on $U$; neither stated independence licenses that replacement. minimal patch (confirmed): corrected statement: Suppose that $U$ is multivariate regularly varying and Assumption~\ref{assump:exo}, \ref{assump:iid}, \ref{assump:f_homo} hold. Suppose in addition that $\alpha<\beta$, $0<p(X)<1$ almost surely, and, for $d\in\{0,1\}$, $\mathbb E[Y(d)\mid X,U,D]=\mathbb E[Y(d)\mid X,U]$ almost surely. Suppose that $\mathbb E[\|Y(d)\|\mid\\|U\\|>t]<\infty$ for both treatments and all sufficiently large $t$. Then we have \begin{align*} \theta^{\tmop{NETE}} &= \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb E[g(X,1,U/\\|U\\|)-g(X,0,U/\\|U\\|)\mid\\|U\\|>t]\cdot\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb E[\\|U\\|^{\alpha}/t^{\alpha}\mid\\|U\\|>t]. \end{align*} · proof change: Write $R_U=\\|U\\|$, $S_U=U/\\|U\\|$, and $\bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)$; the last quantity is finite for sufficiently large $t$ and tends to zero. Independence of $U$ from $(X,D)$ gives $\mathbb P(D=1\mid X,U)=p(X)$. Positivity, consistency, and conditional mean exogeneity given $(X,U)$ therefore give, for both treatments, \[ f(X,d,U)=\mathbb E[Y(d)\mid X,U,D=d]=\mathbb E[Y(d)\mid X,U]. \] The assumed absolute tail means justify taking conditional expectations on $\{R_U>t\}$. Homogeneity at the realized radius yields \[ \frac{f(X,1,U)-f(X,0,U)}{R_U^{\alpha}}=g(X,1,S_U)-g(X,0,S_U)+\varepsilon(X,U),\qquad \|\varepsilon(X,U)\|\leq2\bar e(t)\quad\hbox{on }\{R_U>t\}. \] Consequently, \[ \left\|\mathbb E\left[\frac{Y(1)-Y(0)}{t^{\alpha}}\mid R_U>t\right]-\mathbb E\left[(g(X,1,S_U)-g(X,0,S_U))(R_U/t)^{\alpha}\mid R_U>t\right]\right\|\leq2\bar e(t)\mathbb E[(R_U/t)^{\alpha}\mid R_U>t]. \] We next establish that the radial moment tends to $\beta/(\beta-\alpha)$. For every real $a<\beta$, the identity $x^a=1+\int_1^x a r^{a-1}\,\tmop{dr}$, together with absolute integrability of the integral, gives \[ \mathbb E[(R_U/t)^a\mid R_U>t]=1+\int_1^{\infty}a r^{a-1}\frac{\mathbb P(R_U>tr)}{\mathbb P(R_U>t)}\,\tmop{dr}. \] For $a=0$ the integral is zero; for $a<0$ its absolute integrability follows from $\int_1^{\infty}\|a\|r^{a-1}\,\tmop{dr}=1$.  Note that $\\| U \\|$ is also regularly varying. By Potter's theorem \cite[Theorem 1.56]{bingham1989regular}, for any $\epsilon >0$ and sufficiently large $t$, we have \begin{align*}   \frac{P (\\| U \\| > {rt})}{P (\\| U \\| > t)} & \leqslant 2 r^{-\beta + \epsilon} . \end{align*}  For $0<a<\beta$, choose $0<\epsilon<\beta-a$. The Potter bound dominates the absolute integrand by $2a r^{a-\beta+\epsilon-1}$, whose integral is finite. For $a<0$, the bound by $\|a\|r^{a-1}$ also permits dominated convergence. Regular variation thus gives, for every $a<\beta$, \[ \mathbb E[(R_U/t)^a\mid R_U>t]\longrightarrow1+\int_1^{\infty}a r^{a-\beta-1}\,\tmop{dr}=\frac{\beta}{\beta-a}. \] In particular, the radial moments with $a=\alpha$ are bounded, and the preceding homogeneity error tends to zero. Define \[ G(s)=\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)],\qquad h_t(r,s)=G(s)r^{\alpha}. \] Independence of $X$ and $U$ shows that the remaining conditional expectation is $\mathbb E[h_t(R_U/t,S_U)\mid R_U>t]$. Note that by Assumption \ref{assump:f_homo}, $g$ is a continuous function on a compact set and thus is bounded by some constant $C > 0$. Thus $\|G(s)\|\leq2C$, and $G$ is continuous. If $\alpha\leq0$, then $\|h_t(r,s)\|\leq2C$ for $r\geq1$. If $\alpha>0$, choose $p>1$ with $p\alpha<\beta$. The radial moment calculation gives \[ \sup_{t\geq t_0}\mathbb E[\|G(S_U)(R_U/t)^{\alpha}\|^p\mid R_U>t]\leq(2C)^p\sup_{t\geq t_0}\mathbb E[(R_U/t)^{p\alpha}\mid R_U>t]<\infty. \] This establishes uniform integrability in both cases. Definition~\ref{def:mvarying} gives conditional weak convergence of $(R_U/t,S_U)$ to the product of a Pareto radial law with survival $r^{-\beta}$ for $r\geq1$ and the spectral probability measure $S$. For a bounded truncation of $G(s)r^{\alpha}$, weak convergence gives convergence of expectations. Uniform integrability makes the truncation errors tend to zero uniformly, so \[ \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb E[G(S_U)(R_U/t)^{\alpha}\mid R_U>t]=\left(\int G(s)\,S(\tmop{ds})\right)\frac{\beta}{\beta-\alpha}. \] Weak convergence of the angular marginal and bounded continuity of $G$ also give \[ \lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb E[g(X,1,S_U)-g(X,0,S_U)\mid R_U>t]=\int G(s)\,S(\tmop{ds}). \] Combining these two limits with the vanishing homogeneity error proves the stated product formula. · contribution narrowed (accepted by the owner): Identification for distributions that satisfy exogeneity given $X$ but violate conditional mean exogeneity given $(X,U)$, distributions with a degenerate true propensity, the range $\alpha\geq\beta$, and counterfactuals without finite absolute tail means. ／ [note, F4] [statement-wrong] 'g being L-Lipschitz on this domain' admits two formal readings. (A) Per treatment: for each d, \|g(x,d,u) − g(x',d,u')\| ≤ L(‖x−x'‖_1 + ‖u−u'‖_1) on B_R × S^{d−1}. (B) Joint in (x,d,u): \|g(x,d,u) − g(x',d',u')\| ≤ L(‖x−x'‖_1 + \|d−d'\| + ‖u−u'‖_1), which additionally forces \|g(x,1,u) − g(x,0,u)\| ≤ L. LipschitzG realizes (B). NL edit to pin it: say either 'for each d, g(·,d,·) is L-Lipschitz on B_R × S^{d−1}' or 'g is L-Lipschitz jointly in (x,d,u), with d in {0,1} as a real coordinate'. (rewind rejected by the orchestrator on prop:id: The author settled the reading (session, 2026-10-09): in Assumption~\ref{assump:f_homo}, ``$g$ is $L$-Lipschitz'' is per treatment. For each $d\in\{0,1\}$, $g(\cdot,d,\cdot)$ is $L$-Lipschitz on $B_R\times S^{d-1}$ under the $\ell_1$ norm: $\|g(x,d,u)-g(x^\prime,d,u^\prime)\|\le L(\\|x-x^\prime\\|_1+\\|u-u^\prime\\|_1)$. Nothing is assumed across the two treatments; in particular $\|g(x,1,u)-g(x,0,u)\|\le L$ is not assumed. The Lean statement is being changed to this reading.) |

| statement | \label{thm:rate}Suppose that Assumption \ref{assump:exo}, \ref{assump:overlap}, \ref{assump:iid}, \ref{assump:f_homo}, \ref{assump:mvary} hold, $\alpha < \beta$, where $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are defined in \cref{assump:f_homo} and \cref{assump:mvary} respectively. Furthermore, for any fixed $t$, with probability at least $1 - \delta$, \begin{align*} \|p (X) - \hat{p} (X) \| \leqslant R_p (n, \delta) & , \| \hat{\alpha}_{n} - \alpha\| \leqslant R_\alpha(n,\delta),\\ \|\mathbb{E}[Y/\\|U\\|^{\alpha} \mid X, D, U /\\|U\\|, \\|U\\|> t] &- \hat{g} (X, D, U /\\|U\\|) \| \leqslant R_g (n_t, \delta), \end{align*} where $n_t = \sum_{i = 1}^{n/2} I (\\|U_i \\|> t)$ and $R_p, R_g, R_\alpha$ are estimation errors that are monotonically decreasing with respect to sample size. Then, with probability at least $1 - \delta, \delta \in (0, 1 / 2)$, we have \begin{align} \left\| {{\widehat{\theta }_{n, t}^{\text{DR} }} } - \theta^{NETE} \right\| & \leqslant O (\sqrt{R_p (n/2, \delta)R_g ({n_t}, \delta)} + t^{\beta / 2} n^{-1 / 2}+ \log (1 / \delta) n^{- 1 / (2 +\beta)} \notag\\ & \quad\quad \quad \quad + t^{- \min \{1, \beta\}} + t^{- \beta s / (1 - 2 s)} + \log(t)R_\alpha(n,\delta)+ e (t)).\label{eq:rate_dr} \end{align} and \begin{align} \left\| {{\widehat{\theta }_{n, t}^{\text{IPW}}} } - \theta^{NETE} \right\| & \leqslant O (R_p (n/2, \delta) +t^{\beta / 2} n^{- 1 / 2} + \log (1 / \delta) n^{- 1 / (2 + \beta)} \notag\\ &\quad\quad \quad \quad + t^{- \min \{1, \beta\}} + t^{- \beta s / (1 - 2 s)}+ \log(t)R_\alpha(n,\delta) + e (t)). \label{eq:rate_ipw} \end{align} |
