| findings | [note, V1.5] the corrected statement stands as one statement; the referee would state it as a general statement with corollaries: Keep the general score-dependent product bounds as the main statement and separate the finite-variance specialization, which additionally requires the second-moment bound, $t\geq\exp(1)$, $a\log t\leq1$, and $Q_n\leq h/2$. ／ [note, V1.5] pinned reading: The existential cutoff $t_W$ is chosen sufficiently large for Lemmas~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} and~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw} and so that $\sup_{r\geq t_W}e(r)\leq1$ and $\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>tr)/\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>t)\leq4r^{-\beta}$ for every $t\geq t_W$ and $r\geq1$. (The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate} invokes the first-factor lemmas' sufficiently-large-threshold conclusions, uses the radial moment inequality with $K=4/(\beta-a_0)$, and bounds $G_t$ by $C+1$. The stated Pareto-type radial law and $e(t)\to0$ permit this enlargement of the existential cutoff.) ／ [refuted, V1.5] The nuisance inequalities could concern only random evaluation arguments or hold simultaneously over their support; the proof's applications of the first-factor lemmas determine the latter reading. Under that stronger reading, the following defects remain. (a) The opening identification equality is false: exogeneity conditional on covariates and independence of the noise from treatment do not imply exogeneity conditional on the noise. (b) The asserted sampling rate has no hypothesis controlling normalized outcome fluctuations. (c) The radial-moment inversion is not uniformly Lipschitz near its pole, and monotone estimation-error bounds need not approach zero. (d) The exponent contribution cannot generally be replaced by $\log(t)R_\alpha(n,\delta)$: its reproduced form contains $t^a-1+Kat^a$. (e) The homogeneity contribution requires $\bar e(t)$, rather than the point value $e(t)$. (f) The angular rate omits a necessary logarithm when $\beta=1$. (g) A nuisance event with failure probability $\delta$ leaves no probability budget for evaluation error; sample-size monotonicity does not compare error bounds at different confidence tolerances. (h) The proof also absorbs the logarithmic factors and the different regimes of Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate} into a rate that this lemma does not supply. These are statement failures in (a)--(g), with the false proof steps identified below; (h) is an additional invalid conversion of the available dependency bound. witness: Common setup: take $m=d=d_z=d_u=2$, $A=I_2$, independent Pareto type-II components $Z_1,Z_2$ with index $\beta$, and $U=Z$. Choose $l=1/2$, $u=2$, $\sigma=1/2$, $s=2/5$, $\xi=1/2$, and $\zeta=1$. The nominal density belongs to the stated model class. Take independent bounded covariates, and use positive, nonincreasing nuisance bounds; exact errors can be bounded by $q^{-10}$ or $(q+1)^{-10}$. Except in (f), use $\beta=2$. Each example has a nonzero NETE.  (a) Let $r_0$ be a radial median, $B=I\{\\|U\\|>r_0\}$, and let $V$ be an independent fair Bernoulli variable. Set $D=B\mathbin{\oplus}V$, with $\oplus$ denoting exclusive-or. Let $\varepsilon$ be independent standard Gaussian noise and set $Y(d)=2d+V+\varepsilon$. Because $B$ and $V$ are independent fair Bernoulli variables, $D$ is independent of both $U$ and $V$. Consequently the printed exogeneity and consistency assumptions hold. For $\\|U\\|>r_0$, however, the observed conditional mean is $f(x,d,U)=1+d$. Thus $\alpha=0$, $g(x,d,u)=1+d$, and one may take $e(r)=I\{r\le r_0\}$. The true treatment contrast is always $2$, so $\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}=2$, whereas $\eta\mu=1$. With $\hat p=1/2$, $\hat g=1+d$, and $\hat\alpha_n=0$, both estimators converge to $1$ along $t=n^{1/4}$. The regression-error inequality holds uniformly with, for example, $R_g(q,\delta)=2+(q+1)^{-10}$, while the displayed right sides approach zero. The false step is the opening equality in the theorem's proof.  (b) Instead take independent $D\sim\text{Ber}(1/2)$ and $Y(d)=d+\varepsilon$, where $\varepsilon$ is symmetric stable noise with characteristic function $\mathbb E[\exp(\mathrm{i}v\varepsilon)]=\exp(-\|v\|^{3/2})$. Its absolute first moment is finite. Here $\alpha=0$, $g=d$, $e=0$, and $\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}=1$. Use exact nuisances and $\hat\alpha_n=0$, so $\hat\mu_n=1$. Conditional on $M=\|\mathcal I\|>0$, the DR error has exactly the distribution $2M^{-1/3}\varepsilon$. At $t=n^{1/4}$, $M/n^{1/2}\to1$ in probability. The error therefore has scale $n^{-1/6}$, whereas the claimed right side is $O(n^{-1/4})$. The IPW estimator has the same stable term and an additional smaller binomial term. Both claimed probability bounds fail. The false step is substituting $t^{\beta/2}n^{-1/2}$ for the unrestricted sampling contribution of the first-factor lemmas.  (c) Take $Y(d)=d\\|U\\|$, $\alpha=1$, $g=d$, $e=0$, and $\hat\alpha_n=2-n^{-1}$. The exponent inequality holds with the decreasing bound $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=1+n^{-1}$. Bounded fitted regressions satisfy the regression inequality with a constant bound. For any fixed $t>2$, the IPW first factor converges to the positive number $\mathbb E[\\|U\\|^{-1}\mid\\|U\\|>t]$. The full-input Hill estimator in the algorithm converges to $1/2$. Hence $1-(2-n^{-1})\hat\gamma_n\to0$ in probability and $\|\hat\mu_n\|\to\infty$ in probability. Its denominator is nonzero almost surely at each sample size. The IPW error diverges although the asserted right side stays bounded. The exact inversion is $\|\hat\mu_n-\mu\|=\|\hat\gamma_n(\hat\alpha_n-\alpha)+\alpha(\hat\gamma_n-\gamma)\|/(\|1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n\|\,\|1-\alpha\gamma\|)$; the proof drops its uncontrolled first denominator.  (d) Take $Y(d)=d$, $\alpha=0$, $g=d$, $e=0$, and $\hat\alpha_n=-a_n$, where $a_n=(\log(n+2))^{-1/2}$. Use exact propensity and a bounded fitted regression, whose error is bounded by $R_g(q,\delta)=2+(q+1)^{-10}$. Set $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=a_n$ and $t=n^{1/4}$. The IPW first factor divided by $t^{a_n}$ converges to $1$, and $\hat\mu_n=(1+a_n\hat\gamma_n)^{-1}\to1$. Thus the estimator grows like $\exp(a_n\log n/4)$, while the claimed exponent contribution is only $a_n\log n/4$. The approximation $t^a-1=O(a\log t)$ is false without a bound on $a\log t$.  (e) Define $b(r)=1/\log r$ on the disjoint intervals $[2\cdot4^j,3\cdot4^j]$, $j\ge1$, and zero elsewhere. Let $Y(d)=d(1+b(\\|U\\|))$, $\alpha=0$, $g=d$, and $e(r)=b(r)$. Use $\hat p=1/2$, $\hat g=d$, $\hat\alpha_n=0$, and $R_g(q,\delta)=1+(q+1)^{-10}$. These nuisance inequalities hold uniformly. At $t_j=4^j$ and $n_j=t_j^4$, $e(t_j)=0$, but regular variation gives a fixed positive conditional probability of $\\|U\\|\in[2t_j,3t_j]$. Consequently $\mathbb E[b(\\|U\\|)\mid\\|U\\|>t_j]\ge c_0/\log t_j$ for some $c_0>0$. Both estimators have this bias and sampling error $O_{\mathbb P}(t_j^{-1})$. Their target is $1$, while the claimed right sides are $O(t_j^{-1})$. The false step replaces an error integrated over radii exceeding $t$ by its value at $t$.  (f) Use $\beta=1$, put $h(u)=u_1u_2$ on the positive unit sphere, and take $Y(d)=d(1+h(U/\\|U\\|))$. Then $\alpha=0$, $g(x,d,u)=d(1+h(u))$, $e=0$, and the spectral measure is supported on the coordinate axes, giving $\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}=1$. With exact nuisances, the DR estimator averages $1+h(U/\\|U\\|)$. Integrating the nominal density over $Z_1\in[t,2t]$ and $Z_2\in[1,t]$ gives $\mathbb E[h(U/\\|U\\|)I\{\\|U\\|>t\}]\ge c_1t^{-2}\log t$, while $\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>t)=O(t^{-1})$. Its positive bias is therefore at least $c_2t^{-1}\log t$. At $t=n^{1/3}$, bounded-score sampling fluctuations are $O_{\mathbb P}(n^{-1/3})$, but the bias exceeds every constant multiple of the claimed $n^{-1/3}$ rate with probability approaching one. The IPW estimator has the same bias. The false step drops the logarithm explicitly retained in the angular bounds of the first-factor lemmas.  (g) Take $Y(d)=d+\varepsilon$ with independent standard Gaussian noise, $\alpha=0$, and exact supplied exponent. Let a training-covariate event have probability $\delta_0=1/4$, independently of evaluation observations. Off that event set $\hat p=1/2$, $\hat g(x,1,u)=1$, and $\hat g(x,0,u)=0$. On it set $\hat p=1/4$, $\hat g(x,1,u)=2$, and $\hat g(x,0,u)=0$. For each tolerance $\varepsilon$, choose $R_p(q,\varepsilon)=q^{-10}+\tfrac14 I\{\varepsilon<1/4\}$ and $R_g(q,\varepsilon)=(q+1)^{-10}+I\{\varepsilon<1/4\}$. The stipulated nuisance guarantees hold jointly and uniformly, with bounds decreasing in sample size. At $\delta=\delta_0$ and $t=n^{1/4}$, the claimed right side is $O(n^{-1/4})$. On the bad training event the DR estimator converges to zero instead of its target $1$. On the good event, $n^{1/4}(\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\text{DR}}-1)$ converges to a Gaussian with variance $4$. For every finite bound constant, the limiting failure probability is therefore strictly greater than $\delta_0$: it includes the full bad-event probability and a positive Gaussian tail on the good event. Replacing confidence tolerances by smaller ones changes the nuisance bounds and cannot be justified by their sample-size monotonicity.  (h) For fixed $\delta$, the radial bound supplied by Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate} has order $(\log((8/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/(2+\beta)}$ when $\beta>1$, $(\log n\,\log((8/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/3}$ when $\beta=1$, and $(\log((8/\delta)\log n)/n)^{1/3}$ when $\beta<1$. Each divided by the proof's asserted $\log(1/\delta)n^{-1/(2+\beta)}$ tends to infinity. Thus the displayed conversion cannot be obtained by absorbing constants; the reproduced product bound must retain the available radial regimes and factors. minimal patch (confirmed): corrected statement: Suppose that Assumption \ref{assump:exo}, \ref{assump:overlap}, \ref{assump:iid}, \ref{assump:f_homo}, \ref{assump:mvary} hold, $\alpha < \beta$, where $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are defined in \cref{assump:f_homo} and \cref{assump:mvary} respectively. Suppose in addition that the conditions of Proposition~\ref{prop:id} hold. Suppose that the fitted propensity, regression and exponent are measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data, denoted together by $\mathcal T$, and that $\mathbb E[\|Y/\\|U\\|^{\hat\alpha_n}\|\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t]<\infty$ on each joint nuisance event. Furthermore, for any fixed $t$, with probability at least $1 -   \delta$,   \begin{align*}     \|p (X) - \hat{p} (X) \| \leqslant R_p (n, \delta) & ,  \| \hat{\alpha}_{n} - \alpha\| \leqslant R_\alpha(n,\delta),\\     \|\mathbb{E}[Y/\\|U\\|^{\alpha} \mid X, D, U /\\|U\\|, \\|U\\|> t] &- \hat{g} (X, D, U /\\|U\\|)     \| \leqslant R_g (n_t, \delta),   \end{align*}      where $n_t = \sum_{i = 1}^{n/2} I (\\|U_i \\|> t)$ and $R_p, R_g, R_\alpha$ are estimation   errors that are monotonically decreasing with respect to sample size. Fix $\delta\in(0,1/2)$ and a deterministic threshold $t$. Run the adaptive Hill selection in Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} at tolerance $\delta/6$, and write its full-input output as $\hat\gamma_n=\widetilde\gamma_n$. Put $\pi_t=\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>t)$, $M=\|\mathcal I\|$, $\bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)$, and $G_t=C+\bar e(t)$, where $C$ bounds $\|g\|$ on its compact domain. Use zero for each first-factor estimate when $M=0$. For a generic evaluation observation put $S_U=U/\\|U\\|$ and define \[ \begin{aligned} \psi^{\mathrm{DR}}={}&\hat g(X,1,S_U)-\hat g(X,0,S_U)\\ &+\frac{D-\hat p(X)}{\hat p(X)(1-\hat p(X))}\left(\frac{Y}{\\|U\\|^{\hat\alpha_n}}-\hat g(X,D,S_U)\right),\\ \psi^{\mathrm{IPW}}={}&\frac{Y}{\\|U\\|^{\hat\alpha_n}}\left(\frac{D}{\hat p(X)}-\frac{1-D}{1-\hat p(X)}\right). \end{aligned} \] For each $q\in\{\mathrm{DR},\mathrm{IPW}\}$, choose $B_q>0$ using only $\mathcal T$, and put \[ \begin{aligned} v_t^q(B_q)&=\mathbb E[(\psi^q)^2I\{\|\psi^q\|\leq B_q\}\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t],\\ b_t^q(B_q)&=\mathbb E[\|\psi^q\|I\{\|\psi^q\|>B_q\}\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t],\\ \mathcal S_t^q&=\sqrt{\frac{12v_t^q(B_q)}{\delta\max\{M,1\}}}+\left(1+\frac{12}{\delta}\right)b_t^q(B_q). \end{aligned} \] Fix $a_0\in(0,\beta)$ and put \[ \begin{aligned} a&=R_\alpha(n,\delta/12),\qquad K=\frac{4}{\beta-a_0},\\ \mathcal A_t&=2LC_W\left((\log t)^k t^{-\min\{1,\beta\}}+t^{-\beta s/(1-2s)}\right),\\ \mathcal E_{\mathrm{DR}}={}&\mathcal S_t^{\mathrm{DR}}+\frac2c R_p(n/2,\delta/12)R_g(n_t,\delta/12)\\ &+2\bar e(t)+\mathcal A_t+\frac{2G_t}{c}(t^a-1+Kat^a),\\ \mathcal E_{\mathrm{IPW}}={}&\mathcal S_t^{\mathrm{IPW}}+\frac{2G_t}{c}R_p(n/2,\delta/12)\\ &+2\bar e(t)+\mathcal A_t+\frac{2G_t}{c}(t^a-1+Kat^a). \end{aligned} \] Suppose moreover, as in Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, that the angular Wasserstein term satisfies the bound that Proposition 3.1 of \citet{zhang2023wasserstein} gives for this Pareto-type model, which is taken from that work and not proved here: there are constants $C_W>0$, $k\geq0$ and $t_W\geq1$ such that \[ W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|})\leq C_W(\log t)^k t^{-\min\{1,\beta\}}+C_W t^{-\beta s/(1-2s)}\qquad(t\geq t_W). \] These are the constants $C_W$ and $k$ in the angular term $\mathcal A_t$ above. Let \[ b_n=C_{\delta/6} \begin{cases} \left(\dfrac{\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&0<\beta<1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log n\,\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&\beta=1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log((48/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/(2+\beta)},&\beta>1, \end{cases} \] where $C_{\delta/6}$ is the constant supplied by Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}. Define \[ \begin{aligned} \gamma&=1/\beta,\qquad h=1-\alpha\gamma>0,\\ Q_n&=a(\gamma+b_n)+\|\alpha\|b_n,\qquad d_n=\|1-\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n\|,\\ \mathcal H_n&= \begin{cases} Q_n/(h d_n),&d_n>0,\\ 1/h,&d_n=0. \end{cases} \end{aligned} \] Use Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete}'s convention $\hat\mu_n=0$ when $d_n=0$. For sufficiently large $n$ and $t\geq t_W$, if $a\leq a_0$ and $n\pi_t\geq16\log(12/\delta)$, then, with probability at least $1-\delta$, we have $M\geq n\pi_t/4$ and \[ \left\|\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}-\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}\right\| \leq \|\hat\mu_n\|\mathcal E_{\mathrm{DR}}+\|\eta\|\mathcal H_n, \] and \[ \left\|\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}\right\| \leq \|\hat\mu_n\|\mathcal E_{\mathrm{IPW}}+\|\eta\|\mathcal H_n, \] where $\eta=\lim_{r\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb E[g(X,1,U/\\|U\\|)-g(X,0,U/\\|U\\|)\mid\\|U\\|>r]$. If moreover, for a constant $V<\infty$ independent of $n,t,\delta$ and every $q\in\{\mathrm{DR},\mathrm{IPW}\}$, $\mathbb E[(\psi^q)^2\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t]\leq V$ on the joint nuisance event, $t\geq\exp(1)$, $a\log t\leq1$ and $Q_n\leq h/2$, then on the same joint success event of probability at least $1-\delta$ we also have \[ \begin{aligned} \|\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}-\theta^{NETE}\|\leq C\biggl(&\sqrt{\frac{V}{\delta n\pi_t}}+R_p(n/2,\delta/12)R_g(n_t,\delta/12)\\ &+\bar e(t)+\mathcal A_t+R_\alpha(n,\delta/12)\log t+b_n\biggr),\\ \|\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\theta^{NETE}\|\leq C\biggl(&\sqrt{\frac{V}{\delta n\pi_t}}+R_p(n/2,\delta/12)\\ &+\bar e(t)+\mathcal A_t+R_\alpha(n,\delta/12)\log t+b_n\biggr), \end{aligned} \] where $C$ does not depend on $n,t,\delta$, and $\pi_t\asymp t^{-\beta}$ makes the first term of order $\sqrt{V/\delta}\,t^{\beta/2}n^{-1/2}$. · proof change: Proposition~\ref{prop:id} applies because its identification and integrability conditions are assumed. The limiting angular contrast is $\eta$, and the limiting radial moment is \[ \mu=\int_1^\infty r^\alpha\beta r^{-\beta-1}\,\tmop{dr}=\frac{\beta}{\beta-\alpha}=\frac1h. \] The integral converges because $\alpha<\beta$. Consequently $\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}=\eta\mu$. Since $\|g\|\leq C$, we also have $\|\eta\|\leq2C$. For either estimator, the product identity gives \[ \widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{estimator}}-\theta^{\tmop{NETE}} =\hat\mu_n(\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{estimator}}-\eta)+\eta(\hat\mu_n-\mu). \]  Let $E_\alpha$ be the joint nuisance event at tolerance $\delta/12$. The nuisance hypothesis gives $\mathbb P(E_\alpha^c)\leq\delta/12$, and on this event $\|\hat\alpha_n-\alpha\|\leq R_\alpha(n,\delta/12)=a$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} at confidence tolerance $\delta/3$, using truncation level $B_{\mathrm{DR}}$. Its internal nuisance tolerance is $(\delta/3)/4=\delta/12$, its exponent bound is $a$, and its count requirement is $n\pi_t\geq16\log(12/\delta)$. It supplies an event $E_{\mathrm{DR}}$ with failure probability at most $\delta/3$ on which $M\geq n\pi_t/4$ and $\|\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}-\eta\|\leq\mathcal E_{\mathrm{DR}}$. Apply Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw} at tolerance $\delta/3$ with $B_{\mathrm{IPW}}$; its sampling coefficients are $12/\delta$ and its nuisance and exponent arguments are also $\delta/12$. It supplies an event $E_{\mathrm{IPW}}$ with failure probability at most $\delta/3$ on which $M\geq n\pi_t/4$ and $\|\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\eta\|\leq\mathcal E_{\mathrm{IPW}}$. The use of $R_p(n/2,\delta/12)$ is permitted by its sample-size monotonicity, since $R_p(n,\delta/12)\leq R_p(n/2,\delta/12)$; no comparison between confidence tolerances is used. Lemma~\ref{lemma:mu_rate}, applied to the full-input Hill selection run at tolerance $\delta/6$, supplies an event $E_\gamma$ with failure probability at most $\delta/6$ on which $\|\hat\gamma_n-\gamma\|\leq b_n$. Thus \[ E=E_\alpha\cap E_{\mathrm{DR}}\cap E_{\mathrm{IPW}}\cap E_\gamma \] satisfies \[ \mathbb P(E^c)\leq\frac{\delta}{12}+\frac{\delta}{3}+\frac{\delta}{3}+\frac{\delta}{6}=\frac{11\delta}{12}\leq\delta. \] This intersection explicitly contains the exponent inequality as well as both first-factor inequalities and the radial inequality. Independence between these events is unnecessary. On $E$, we have $\|\hat\gamma_n\|\leq\gamma+b_n$ and hence \[ \|\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n-\alpha\gamma\| =\|\hat\gamma_n(\hat\alpha_n-\alpha)+\alpha(\hat\gamma_n-\gamma)\| \leq a(\gamma+b_n)+\|\alpha\|b_n=Q_n. \] If $d_n>0$, exact reciprocal subtraction gives \[ \|\hat\mu_n-\mu\| =\frac{\|\hat\alpha_n\hat\gamma_n-\alpha\gamma\|}{h d_n} \leq\frac{Q_n}{h d_n}=\mathcal H_n. \] If $d_n=0$, Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} sets $\hat\mu_n=0$, so \[ \|\hat\mu_n-\mu\|=1/h=\mathcal H_n. \] Thus $\|\hat\mu_n-\mu\|\leq\mathcal H_n$ throughout $E$, without any uniform Lipschitz assertion near the pole.  On $E$, the DR product identity and the triangle inequality give \[ \begin{aligned} \|\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}-\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}\| &\leq\|\hat\mu_n\|\,\|\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{DR}}-\eta\|+\|\eta\|\,\|\hat\mu_n-\mu\|\\ &\leq\|\hat\mu_n\|\mathcal E_{\mathrm{DR}}+\|\eta\|\mathcal H_n. \end{aligned} \] Here Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} supplies the score-dependent sampling term, the product $R_p(n/2,\delta/12)R_g(n_t,\delta/12)$, the tail envelope $2\bar e(t)$, the angular Wasserstein term and the full exponent perturbation. Its exponent calculation uses $\|\hat\alpha_n-\alpha\|\leq a$ on $E_\alpha$ and gives $\mathbb E[\\|U\\|^a-1\mid\\|U\\|>t]\leq t^a-1+Kat^a$ for $K=4/(\beta-a_0)$. No linearization of this expression, replacement of $\bar e(t)$ by $e(t)$, or suppression of angular or radial logarithms is made. The count inequality also holds on $E$.  For IPW, the product identity gives \[ \widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\theta^{\tmop{NETE}} =\hat\mu_n(\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\eta)+\eta(\hat\mu_n-\mu). \] On $E$, Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw} gives $\|\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\eta\|\leq\mathcal E_{\mathrm{IPW}}$, and the reciprocal calculation gives $\|\hat\mu_n-\mu\|\leq\mathcal H_n$. Therefore \[ \begin{aligned} \|\widehat\theta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\theta^{\tmop{NETE}}\| &\leq\|\hat\mu_n\|\,\|\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\mathrm{IPW}}-\eta\|+\|\eta\|\,\|\hat\mu_n-\mu\|\\ &\leq\|\hat\mu_n\|\mathcal E_{\mathrm{IPW}}+\|\eta\|\mathcal H_n. \end{aligned} \] Both bounds and $M\geq n\pi_t/4$ thus hold together with probability at least $1-\delta$.  For the additional assertion, we choose the success events in the preceding proof using the constructions in Lemmas~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} and~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw}. Let $E_M=\{M\geq n\pi_t/4\}$. Its failure probability is at most $\exp(-n\pi_t/16)\leq\delta/12$. For each $q\in\{\mathrm{DR},\mathrm{IPW}\}$ put $Z^q=\psi^q I\{\|\psi^q\|\leq B_q\}$ and $W^q=\psi^q I\{\|\psi^q\|>B_q\}$. Conditional on $\mathcal T$ and positive $M$, Chebyshev's and Markov's inequalities give two events $E_{Z,q}$ and $E_{W,q}$, each with conditional failure probability at most $\delta/12$, on which \[ \left\|M^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I}Z_i^q-\mathbb E[Z^q\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t]\right\|\leq\sqrt{\frac{12v_t^q(B_q)}{\delta M}},\qquad M^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal I}\|W_i^q\|\leq\frac{12b_t^q(B_q)}\delta. \] If $b_t^q(B_q)=0$, the tail variables vanish almost surely. Take $E_{\mathrm{DR}}=E_\alpha\cap E_M\cap E_{Z,\mathrm{DR}}\cap E_{W,\mathrm{DR}}$ and $E_{\mathrm{IPW}}=E_\alpha\cap E_M\cap E_{Z,\mathrm{IPW}}\cap E_{W,\mathrm{IPW}}$. These events give exactly the first-factor conclusions used in the preceding proof, by the two cited lemmas, and each has failure probability at most $\delta/3$. Since their nuisance and count events are shared, their intersection with $E_\gamma$ actually has failure probability at most \[ \delta/12+\delta/12+4\delta/12+\delta/6=8\delta/12. \]  On $E_\alpha$, the additional second-moment assumption gives $\tmop{Var}(\psi^q\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t)\leq V$. Conditional Chebyshev supplies an event $F_q$ whose failure on $E_\alpha\cap E_M$ has probability at most $\delta/12$ and on which \[ \left\|\hat\eta_{n,t}^q-\mathbb E[\psi^q\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t]\right\|\leq\sqrt{\frac{12V}{\delta M}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{48V}{\delta n\pi_t}}. \] The assertion follows directly if $V=0$, because the centered scores then vanish almost surely. Integrating the conditional failure bounds and using a union bound shows that $E_*=E\cap F_{\mathrm{DR}}\cap F_{\mathrm{IPW}}$ has failure probability at most $10\delta/12\leq\delta$. Thus both general bounds and both finite-variance bounds hold on this one event; it contains the exponent inequality explicitly.  The finite-variance alternatives in Lemmas~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} and~\ref{lemma:eta_ipw} give the same conditional-mean bounds on $E_\alpha$, with this sampling term. Write $z=a\log t\in[0,1]$. Convexity of the exponential gives $\exp(z)-1\leq(\exp(1)-1)z$; also $t^a\leq\exp(1)$ and $a\leq a\log t$. Therefore \[ t^a-1+Kat^a\leq(\exp(1)-1+K\exp(1))a\log t. \] For sufficiently large $t$, $\bar e(t)\leq1$, so $G_t$ is bounded by the fixed constant $C+1$. On $E_*$, $d_n\geq h-Q_n\geq h/2>0$, and consequently \[ \|\hat\mu_n\|\leq2/h,\qquad \mathcal H_n\leq2Q_n/h^2, \qquad Q_n\leq\gamma a+(a_0+\|\alpha\|)b_n\leq C_1(a\log t+b_n). \] Here $C_1$ depends only on the fixed model and $a_0$. Substituting the DR mean bound and the finite-variance sampling bound in the DR product identity gives the additional DR display. Substituting the IPW mean bound and its finite-variance sampling bound in the IPW product identity gives the additional IPW display. The factors $2/h$, $\|\eta\|$, $2/c$, the bounded $G_t$ and the displayed exponent constant can all be absorbed into a constant independent of $n,t,\delta$. Finally the radial tail in the stated model is $\pi_t\asymp t^{-\beta}$, which gives the asserted order of the sampling term. The definition of $b_n$ and every nuisance tolerance remain exactly those displayed in the statement. · contribution narrowed (accepted by the owner): The unconditional claim under covariate-only exogeneity is lost, including cases where treatment and potential outcomes become dependent after conditioning on $U$. Fitted nuisances using evaluation observations and fitted-exponent pseudo-outcomes without finite conditional absolute means are excluded. The suggested first-factor bound requires $R_\alpha(n,\delta/12)\leq a_0<\beta$ and enough expected exceedances for its probability guarantee. A universal square-root sampling rate without outcome-tail control, a uniformly bounded reciprocal perturbation near its pole, the linear exponent term without control of $R_\alpha(n,\delta)\log t$, the pointwise homogeneity term $e(t)$, and the logarithm-free angular and radial rates are lost. The nuisance bounds are evaluated at the smaller confidence tolerance $\delta/12$. The appended square-root sampling and linear exponent bounds apply only to scores satisfying the stated conditional second-moment bound, thresholds $t\geq\exp(1)$ with $R_\alpha(n,\delta/12)\log t\leq1$, and perturbations satisfying $Q_n\leq h/2$; the general product bounds still retain the reciprocal denominator and full exponent perturbation outside those additional conditions. |

| statement | \label{cor:rate} Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, further suppose that \begin{align*} R_p (n, \delta) = \Theta (\log (1 / \delta) n^{- 1 / 2}) , R_g (n, \delta) = \Theta (\log (1 / \delta) n^{- 1 / 2}), R_\alpha (n, \delta) = \Theta( \log(1/\delta) n^{-c_\alpha}), \end{align*} for some $c_\alpha > 0 $, the following conclusions hold. \begin{enumerate} \item If $s \in (0, 1 / (2 + \max \{1, \beta\}))$, takes $t_n = \Theta (n^{ (1 - 2 s) \hat{\gamma}_{n}})$, with probability at least $1 - \delta$, we have \begin{align*} \| {{\widehat{\theta }_{n, t}^{\text{DR} }} } - \theta^{NETE} \| & = O (e (t_n) + n^{- s} \log (1 / \delta) + n^{-c_\alpha} \log(n)\log (1 / \delta)) . \end{align*} \item If $s \in [1 / (2 + \max \{1, \beta\}), 1 / 2)$, \ takes $t = \Theta (n^{(\hat{\gamma}_{n} / (1 + 2 \min \{1, \hat{\gamma}_{n} \})})$, with probability at least $1 - \delta$, we have \begin{align*} \| {{\widehat{\theta }_{n, t}^{\text{DR} }} } - \theta^{NETE} \| & = O (e (t_n) + n^{- 1 / (2 + \max \{\beta, 1\})} \log (1 / \delta) + n^{-c_\alpha} \log(n)\log (1 / \delta)) . \end{align*} \end{enumerate} |

| findings | [refuted, V1.5] The core faithfully retains the assumptions and algorithm reference. (a) The unresolved readings are the outcome exponent versus a local tail-index meaning of $\alpha$, and the algorithm's adjacent-ratio statistic versus the proof's common-denominator Hill statistic. The input collection, admissible ranks, and calibration of $l_n$ and $r_n(\delta)$ are not reconciled. (b) Algorithm~\ref{alg:nete} compares candidates through $n$, although its evaluation collection contains at most $n/2$ observations; moreover, its displayed statistic telescopes and lacks the weights required for a Hill estimator. (c) The proof's comparison against every candidate index is not the cited adaptive selection rule and can leave the admissible set empty even after removing unavailable ranks. (d) The asserted second-order tail expansion, and hence $\eta(t)=O(t^{-1/\beta})$, fail at the admissible value $\beta=1$, where a logarithmic term is present. (e) Two additional proof slips can be closed locally: the proportional component tail should be transferred using the original product Pareto law, rather than the shifted component law; and differentiating the displayed survival expansion gives the density coefficient $D(1+1/\beta)$, not $D(1-1/\beta)$. These corrections do not resolve (b)--(d). (f) I also could not close the final removal of the adaptation factor: [Boucheron and Thomas, Corollary 3.12](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1503.05077) retains $\log((2/\delta)\log n)$, whereas the printed rate retains only $\log(2/\delta)$. witness: (b) Take independent Pareto type-II components $Z_1,Z_2$ with $\beta=1$, $U=Z$, $A=I_2$, $m=d=d_z=d_u=2$, $l=1/4$, $u=2$, $\sigma=1/8$, $s=1/4$, $\xi=1/2$, and $\zeta=1$, with independent observations and fixed threshold $t=1$. This exact product law satisfies every stated tail-model restriction. Nevertheless, $M=\|\mathcal I\|\leq n/2$, so the comparison at $i=n$ requires an unavailable order statistic. Independently, the algorithm's display equals $k^{-1}\log(\\|U_{(1)}\\|/\\|U_{(k+1)}\\|)$, whereas the proof's statistic equals $k^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^k j\log(\\|U_{(j)}\\|/\\|U_{(j+1)}\\|)$. They are different random variables. For the permitted choice $l_n=\lceil(\log n)^2\rceil$, every computable algorithmic candidate satisfies $0\leq\hat\gamma_k\leq\log(\max_i\\|U_i\\|/t)/l_n=O_{\mathbb P}(1/\log n)$, incompatible with $\gamma=1$. (c) On the same radial law, even using the proof's full-sample Hill statistic and deleting the unavailable endpoint, choose $l_n=\lceil(\log n)^2\rceil$ and $r_n(\delta)$ of the stated order. Then $\hat\gamma(l_n)\to1$ in probability, while $\hat\gamma(n-1)=\tfrac12\log n+O_{\mathbb P}(1)$ because the minimum radius is of order $n^{-1/2}$. Both comparison tolerances tend to zero. No candidate can satisfy comparisons with both indices, so the printed maximum is empty with probability tending to one. (d) Direct convolution on this exact product law gives $\bar F(t)=2/(t+2)+2\log(t+1)/(t+2)^2=2t^{-1}+(2\log t-4)t^{-2}+o(t^{-2})$. Thus the displayed constant-coefficient second-order expansion is false. Its tail quantile satisfies $U(q)=2q+\log(2q)-2+o(1)$, and the von Mises function has leading term $-\log q/(2q)$, which is not $O(q^{-1})$. This failure persists even under the favorable interpretation that the density calculation's $\alpha$ means $\beta$. minimal patch (confirmed): corrected statement: Under Assumption \ref{assump:iid}, \ref{assump:mvary}, using the standing convention $\\|\cdot\\|=\\|\cdot\\|_1$, let $R_i=\\|U_i\\|_1$ for all $i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and order these full-input radii as $R_{[1]}\geq\cdots\geq R_{[n]}$. For $n\geq2$, define \[ H_n(k)=\frac1k\sum_{j=1}^k\log\frac{R_{[j]}}{R_{[k+1]}},\qquad 1\leq k\leq n-1. \] For $\delta\in(0,1)$, set \[ \begin{aligned} L_n&=\min\{n-1,\lceil100\log n\rceil\},&J_n&=1+\lceil\log n/\log2\rceil,\\ q_n(\delta)&=\log(4J_n/\delta),&c_n(\delta)&=2\sqrt{q_n(\delta)}+\frac{q_n(\delta)}{\sqrt{L_n}},\\ \rho_n(\delta)&=16c_n(\delta). \end{aligned} \] Let \[ \begin{aligned} \widetilde k_n&=\max\bigl\{k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}:\\ &\qquad \|H_n(i)-H_n(k)\|\leq\frac{H_n(i)\rho_n(\delta)}{\sqrt i}\\ &\qquad \text{for every }i\in\{L_n,\ldots,k\}\bigr\},\\ \widetilde\gamma_n&=H_n(\widetilde k_n). \end{aligned} \] For each fixed $\delta\in(0,1)$ and every sufficiently large $n$, with probability at least $1-\delta$, we have \[ \|\gamma-\widetilde\gamma_n\|\leq C_\delta \begin{cases} \left(\dfrac{\log((8/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&0<\beta<1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log n\,\log((8/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/3},&\beta=1,\\ \left(\dfrac{\log((8/\delta)\log n)}{n}\right)^{1/(2+\beta)},&\beta>1, \end{cases} \] where $C_\delta$ may depend on the fixed distribution and on $\delta$.      where $\gamma = 1 / \beta$ is the EVI of $U$. · proof change: We adopt the non-asymptotic analysis of the adaptive Hill estimator for EVI   in {\citep{boucheron2015tail}}. We give the exponential-spacing argument and the confidence calibration explicitly. Write $R=\\|U\\|_1$. Fix a member of $M_K$, put $a_i=\\|A_{\cdot i}\\|_1$, and let $Z_i^0$ be independent nominal Pareto type-II components of index $\beta$. The component density is a positive constant $c_0$ times the nominal product density whenever $\\|z\\|_1>\zeta K^{(1-2s)/\beta}$. Since \[ R=\sum_{i=1}^m a_iZ_i,\qquad l\leq a_i\leq u,\qquad R\leq u\\|Z\\|_1, \] the survival and density of $R$ beyond $u\zeta K^{(1-2s)/\beta}$ are $c_0$ times those of $T=\sum_i a_iZ_i^0$. This transfer uses the original nominal components without shifting their supports.  Put $W_i=a_iZ_i^0$, $V_i=\sum_{j\ne i}W_j$, and $f_i(x)=\beta a_i^\beta(a_i+x)^{-\beta-1}$ for $x\geq0$, extended by zero for $x<0$. Partitioning the convolution integral according to the largest summand, whose ties have probability zero, gives \[ f_T(r)=\sum_i\mathbb E\left[f_i(r-V_i)I\{\max_{j\ne i}W_j\leq r-V_i\}\right]. \] On this event $r-V_i\geq r/m$. If $V_i\leq r/2$, the event holds and the mean value theorem gives $\|f_i(r-V_i)-f_i(r)\|\leq C r^{-\beta-2}V_i$. On the part where $V_i>r/2$, the indicated density is at most $C r^{-\beta-1}$. A union bound using the tails of the $m-1$ summands gives $\mathbb P(V_i>x)\leq Cx^{-\beta}$ for $x\geq1$. Integrating this bound and using $\mathbb E[V_iI\{V_i\leq r/2\}]\leq\mathbb E[\min\{V_i,r/2\}]$ gives \[ \mathbb E[V_iI\{V_i\leq r/2\}]\leq \begin{cases}Cr^{1-\beta},&\beta<1,\\ C\log r,&\beta=1,\\ C,&\beta>1.\end{cases} \] Consequently \[ \left\|f_T(r)-\sum_i f_i(r)\right\| \leq C\sum_i\left(r^{-\beta-2}\mathbb E[V_iI\{V_i\leq r/2\}]+r^{-\beta-1}\mathbb P(V_i>r/2)\right). \] Since $\sum_i f_i(r)=\beta\sum_i a_i^\beta r^{-\beta-1}(1+O(r^{-1}))$, set \[ b_\beta(r)=\begin{cases}r^{-\beta},&\beta<1,\\r^{-1}\log r,&\beta=1,\\r^{-1},&\beta>1,\end{cases}\qquad C_0=c_0\sum_i a_i^\beta>0. \] The actual radial density and survival therefore satisfy, for sufficiently large $r$, \[ f_R(r)=\beta C_0r^{-\beta-1}(1+O(b_\beta(r))),\qquad \bar F_R(r)=C_0r^{-\beta}(1+O(b_\beta(r))). \] The second estimate follows by integrating the first error from $r$ to infinity: the integrals of $r^{-2\beta-1}$, $r^{-3}\log r$, and $r^{-\beta-2}$ have orders $r^{-2\beta}$, $r^{-2}\log r$, and $r^{-\beta-1}$, respectively. Thus no constant-coefficient expansion at $\beta=1$ is required. When $\beta>1$ and a survival expansion with coefficient $D$ is used, differentiation gives density coefficient $D(1+1/\beta)$; the density and survival envelopes just proved avoid differentiating an unspecified remainder.  Let $Q(v)=F_R^{-1}(1-1/v)$ be the radial tail quantile. Its differentiable tail has $Q(v)\sim(C_0v)^{1/\beta}$ and \[ \eta(v)=\frac{vQ'(v)}{Q(v)}-\gamma =\frac{\bar F_R(Q(v))}{Q(v)f_R(Q(v))}-\frac1\beta =O(b_\beta(Q(v))). \] Choose a fixed $v_0>1$ in that tail and define $\bar\eta(v)=\sup_{w\geq\max\{v,v_0\}}\|\eta(w)\|$ for every $v>0$. For sufficiently large $v$ this gives \[ \bar\eta(v)\leq \begin{cases}Cv^{-1},&\beta<1,\\Cv^{-1}\log v,&\beta=1,\\Cv^{-1/\beta},&\beta>1.\end{cases} \] For $y\geq z\geq\log v_0$, integrating the quantile derivative gives \[ \|\log Q(e^y)-\log Q(e^z)-\gamma(y-z)\|\leq\bar\eta(e^z)(y-z). \]  We next prove the simultaneous stochastic bound used for selection. Let $Y_{(1)}\geq\cdots\geq Y_{(n)}$ be decreasing order statistics of independent standard exponential variables, with $Y_{(n+1)}=0$. Their joint density is $n!\exp(-\sum_j y_j)$ on the ordered region. The change of variables $E_j=j(Y_{(j)}-Y_{(j+1)})$ has Jacobian $1/n!$ and $\sum_j y_j=\sum_j E_j$, so the $E_j$ are independent standard exponential variables. The quantile transform represents the ordered radii as $Q(e^{Y_{(j)}})$. Define $B_i=\sum_{j=1}^i(E_j-1)$ and $A_i=i^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^iE_j$ for this argument only.  For $0<\lambda<1$, \[ \log\mathbb E e^{\lambda(E_j-1)}=-\lambda-\log(1-\lambda)\leq\frac{\lambda^2}{2(1-\lambda)},\qquad \log\mathbb E e^{-\lambda(E_j-1)}=\lambda-\log(1+\lambda)\leq\frac{\lambda^2}{2(1-\lambda)}. \] Hence $\exp(\pm\lambda B_i-i\lambda^2/(2(1-\lambda)))$ are nonnegative supermartingales with initial value one. The maximal supermartingale inequality implies \[ \mathbb P\left(\max_{i\leq m}\|B_i\|\geq\sqrt{2mx}+x\right)\leq2e^{-x}. \] Indeed, each one-sided probability is at most $\exp(-\lambda a+m\lambda^2/(2(1-\lambda)))$ for $a=\sqrt{2mx}+x$; taking $\lambda=\sqrt{2x/m}/(1+\sqrt{2x/m})$ makes this bound $e^{-x}$.  Apply this inequality at the ends of the dyadic intervals $2^a\leq i<2^{a+1}$, using $m=\min\{n,2^{a+1}\}$ and $x=q_n(\delta)$. There are at most $J_n$ such intervals. On each interval $\sqrt{2m}/\sqrt i\leq2$. The union bound therefore supplies an event $E$ of probability at least $1-\delta/2$ on which \[ \sqrt i\,\|A_i-1\|\leq2\sqrt{q_n(\delta)}+q_n(\delta)/\sqrt i\leq c_n(\delta),\qquad L_n\leq i\leq n. \] For fixed $\delta$, $c_n(\delta)=O_\delta(\sqrt{\log\log n})$ and $c_n(\delta)/\sqrt{L_n}\to0$.  Write $c_n=c_n(\delta)$ and consider the deterministic index \[ k_* =\max\left\{k\in\{L_n,\ldots,n-1\}:\ \frac{n}{2k}\geq v_0,\quad \sqrt k\,\bar\eta\left(\frac{n}{2k}\right)\leq\gamma c_n\right\}. \] The choices below show that this set is nonempty for all sufficiently large $n$. Put $T_*=e^{Y_{(k_*+1)}}$. The number of exponential observations exceeding $\log(n/(2k_*))$ has binomial law with mean $2k_*$. Its multiplicative lower-tail bound gives \[ \mathbb P\left(T_*<\frac{n}{2k_*}\right)\leq\exp(-k_*/4)\leq\delta/2 \] for sufficiently large $n$, because $k_*\geq L_n$. Indeed, if $B\sim\tmop{Bin}(n,p)$ and $\mu=np$, Markov's inequality gives \[ \mathbb P(B\leq\mu/2)\leq e^{\mu\log2/2}\mathbb E[2^{-B}] =e^{\mu\log2/2}(1-p/2)^n \leq e^{-\mu(1-\log2)/2}\leq e^{-\mu/8}, \] using $\log2\leq3/4$; here $p=2k_*/n$ and $\mu=2k_*$. Let $E_*$ be the complementary event. On $E_*$ all spacings involved in $H_n(i)$ for $i\leq k_*$ lie above $\log v_0$. Since $H_n(i)=i^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^i j\log(R_{[j]}/R_{[j+1]})$ by telescoping, the quantile inequality thus gives \[ \|H_n(i)-\gamma A_i\|\leq\bar\eta(T_*)A_i\leq\frac{\gamma c_n}{\sqrt{k_*}}A_i,\qquad L_n\leq i\leq k_*. \] On $E\cap E_*$, for large enough $n$, $c_n/\sqrt{L_n}\leq1/6$. It follows that \[ \|H_n(i)-\gamma\|\leq\frac{\gamma c_n}{\sqrt i}+\frac{\gamma c_n}{\sqrt{k_*}}\left(1+\frac{c_n}{\sqrt i}\right) \leq\frac{3\gamma c_n}{\sqrt i},\qquad \gamma/2\leq H_n(i)\leq3\gamma/2. \] For every $L_n\leq i\leq k\leq k_*$, \[ \|H_n(i)-H_n(k)\|\leq\frac{6\gamma c_n}{\sqrt i} \leq\frac{H_n(i)\rho_n(\delta)}{\sqrt i}. \] Thus $k_*$ qualifies for the displayed prefix rule, and its selected index $\widetilde k_n$ is at least $k_*$. The set in that rule is nonempty almost surely because $k=L_n$ only compares with itself. The comparison at $i=k_*$ now yields \[ \|\widetilde\gamma_n-\gamma\|\leq\|H_n(k_*)-\gamma\|+\frac{H_n(k_*)\rho_n(\delta)}{\sqrt{k_*}} \leq\frac{27\gamma c_n}{\sqrt{k_*}}. \] The two events used have total failure probability at most $\delta$; independence between them is unnecessary.  To obtain the displayed rates, when $\beta\ne1$ put $p=\min\{1,1/\beta\}$ and choose a sufficiently small fixed $a_*>0$. Let \[ k_0=\left\lfloor a_* n^{2p/(1+2p)}c_n^{2/(1+2p)}\right\rfloor. \] For all sufficiently large $n$, $k_0\geq L_n$, $k_0<n/(2v_0)$, and \[ \sqrt{k_0}\,\bar\eta(n/(2k_0))\leq C2^p k_0^{p+1/2}n^{-p}\leq\gamma c_n \] by making $a_*$ small. Thus $k_*\geq k_0$, and \[ \frac{c_n}{\sqrt{k_*}}\leq C n^{-p/(1+2p)}c_n^{2p/(1+2p)} \leq C_\delta\left(\frac{\log((8/\delta)\log n)}n\right)^{p/(1+2p)}. \] Here $c_n^2\leq C_\delta\log((8/\delta)\log n)$ for sufficiently large $n$. For $\beta<1$ the exponent is $1/3$, and for $\beta>1$ it is $1/(2+\beta)$.  When $\beta=1$, take \[ k_0=\left\lfloor a_*n^{2/3}\left(\frac{c_n}{\log n}\right)^{2/3}\right\rfloor. \] Again $k_0\geq L_n$ and $k_0<n/(2v_0)$ eventually, and \[ \sqrt{k_0}\,\bar\eta(n/(2k_0))\leq Ck_0^{3/2}n^{-1}\log n\leq\gamma c_n \] for sufficiently small $a_*$. Hence $k_*\geq k_0$ and \[ \frac{c_n}{\sqrt{k_*}}\leq C n^{-1/3}(c_n^2\log n)^{1/3} \leq C_\delta\left(\frac{\log n\,\log((8/\delta)\log n)}n\right)^{1/3}. \] This proves the middle case on the same event of probability at least $1-\delta$, and completes the proof. · narrowed: The guarantee for the printed evaluation-exceedance adjacent-ratio estimate and the selector comparing every $i\in\{l_n,\ldots,n\}$ is replaced by a guarantee for a different full-input Hill estimate with prefix selection. The logarithm-free radial bound is lost, and the radial exponent $1/(2+\beta)$ is replaced by $1/3$ for $0<\beta<1$. |

| statement | \label{lemma:eta_rate}Undet the assumption of Theorem \ref{thm:rate}, with probability at least $1 - \delta$, we have \begin{align*} \| \hat{\eta}_{n, t}^{\tmop{DR}} - \eta \| & \leqslant O (\sqrt{R_p (n/2, \delta) R_g (t^{- \beta} n, \delta)} + t^{\beta / 2} n^{- 1 / 2} + t^{- \min \{1, \beta\}} + t^{- \beta s / (1 - 2 s)} + e (t) + \log (t) R_{\alpha} (n,\delta)) . \end{align*} |

| findings | [note, V1.5] the corrected statement stands as one statement; the referee would state it as a general statement with corollaries: Keep the general truncated-score DR bound as the main statement; separate the angular-rate specialization under the additional Wasserstein hypothesis and the sampling specialization under finite conditional score variance into blocks. ／ [refuted, V1.5] The reference to Theorem~\ref{thm:rate} imports its hypotheses, not its conclusion, so the lint observation about an absent theorem edge is dismissed. Exogeneity, overlap, the three i.i.d. blocks, and the three Pareto-model blocks are all inherited; the other eight lint observations are dismissed. The setting faithfully distinguishes training and evaluation counts. Ambiguity remains between the displayed covariate-law class and the noise-law interpretation, and between equal and distinct component dimensions. I use the binding limiting angular meaning of $\eta$. Reproduction yields six independent failures: (a) the sampling step asserts a square-root rate without controlling the variance of the normalized score; (b) the homogeneity remainder over exceedances is controlled by an average of $e(\\|U\\|)$ or its tail supremum, not by $e(t)$; (c) at $\beta=1$, the angular bias can be $\Theta(t^{-1}\log t)$, so the logarithm omitted from [Zhang et al., Proposition 3.1](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2312.09862) is material; (d) exponent error is not uniformly linear in $R_\alpha(n,\delta)\log t$ without a smallness restriction; (e) monotonicity does not permit replacing a random training count by $nt^{-\beta}$ inside an arbitrary $R_g$; and (f) separate nuisance and evaluation guarantees do not retain failure probability $\delta$ with the same nuisance-bound arguments. The evaluation sampling term uses $\|\mathcal I\|$, rather than the training $n_t$. Both counts can concentrate, but this does not repair (e). Correcting the proof's triangle-inequality signs, conditioning notation, Lipschitz factor, and Bernstein lower-tail sign leaves all six counterexamples intact. witness: For the following examples, take independent $X\sim\text{Unif}([0,1])$, $D\sim\text{Ber}(1/2)$, and independent Pareto type-II components $Z_1,Z_2$, with $U=AZ$ and $m=d=d_z=d_u=2$. Fix $l=1/4$, $u=2$, $\sigma=1/8$, $\xi=1/2$, and $\zeta=1$. Both $A=I_2$ and $A=I_2/2$ satisfy the matrix conditions, and the exact product density satisfies every component-class restriction. All potential outcomes below satisfy consistency and exogeneity. Oracle fitted nuisances are admissible regression outputs, including regression over singleton function classes. Except where specified otherwise, use exact exponent and nuisance functions with positive decreasing bounds $R_p(n,\delta)=(1+n)^{-10}$, $R_g(q,\delta)=(1+q)^{-10}$, and $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=(1+n)^{-10}$.  (a) Set $A=I_2$, $\beta=3$, $\alpha=1$, $s=1/4$, and $Y(d)=\\|U\\|(d+\epsilon)$, where independent symmetric $\epsilon$ has characteristic function $\mathbb E[\exp(iv\epsilon)]=\exp(-\|v\|^{3/2})$. Then $g(x,d,s)=d$, $e(t)=0$, and $\eta=1$. With $\hat p=1/2$ and $\hat g(x,d,s)=d$, the DR score is $1+2(2D-1)\epsilon$. Conditional on $M=\|\mathcal I\|$, its average error has the law of $2M^{-1/3}\epsilon$, despite its finite first moment. At $t=n^{1/5}$, $M$ concentrates at order $n^{2/5}$, so the error has scale $n^{-2/15}$, while the entire printed right-hand side is $O(n^{-1/5})$. Coverage of any fixed multiple of that bound tends to zero. The false step is the unsupported square-root concentration assertion, not a zero-variance or undefined-mean case.  (b) Keep $\beta=3$, $\alpha=1$, $s=1/4$, and $A=I_2$. Write $h(r)=\sin^2(\log r)/(1+\|\log r\|)$ and set $Y(1)=\\|U\\|[1+h(\\|U\\|)]$, $Y(0)=0$. Exact homogeneity bounds hold with $g(x,1,s)=1$, $g(x,0,s)=0$, and positive $e(r)=h(r)+(1+r)^{-2}$. Use the oracle conditional normalized-outcome regressions. At $t_j=\exp(j\pi)$, $e(t_j)=O(t_j^{-2})$, but regular variation gives $\mathbb E[h(\\|U\\|)\mid\\|U\\|>t_j]=\Theta((\log t_j)^{-1})$. Taking even $n_j$ of order $t_j^{10}$ makes sampling error negligible; the printed right-hand side is $O(t_j^{-1})$, whereas the estimator's bias has order $(\log t_j)^{-1}$. The step bounding the homogeneity contribution by $2e(t)$ is false because the assumption bounds the error at each realized radius separately.  (c) Set $\beta=1$, $\alpha=1/2$, $s=2/5$, $A=I_2$, and $Y(d)=d\\|U\\|^{1/2}[1+\min\{U_1,U_2\}/\\|U\\|]$. Then $e(t)=0$, $g(x,1,s)=1+\min\{s_1,s_2\}$, $g(x,0,s)=0$, and the limiting spectral measure is supported on the two coordinate axes, giving $\eta=1$. Oracle nuisances make the score exactly $1+\min\{U_1,U_2\}/\\|U\\|$. Direct integration of the product density gives $\mathbb E[\min\{U_1,U_2\}/\\|U\\|\mid\\|U\\|>t]=\Theta((\log t)/t)$. For example, integrating over $1\leq Z_1\leq t/2$ and $t\leq Z_2\leq2t$ gives a lower bound of this order, since $\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>t)\sim2/t$. With $n$ of order $t^5$, sampling error is $O(t^{-2})$ and the printed bound is $O(t^{-1})$. The actual angular bias therefore violates the conclusion; $\beta=1$ is an interior value of the stated tail-index domain.  (d) Set $\beta=3$, $\alpha=1$, $s=1/4$, $A=I_2$, and $Y(d)=d\\|U\\|$. Let $\kappa_n=(\log(n+\exp(1)))^{-1/2}$, $\hat\alpha_n=1-\kappa_n$, and $R_\alpha(n,\delta)=\kappa_n$. Use $\hat p=1/2$ and $\hat g(x,d,s)=d$, obtainable by regression over that singleton class; the displayed nuisance assumption concerns the true-exponent regression and is satisfied exactly. The score is $1+2D(\\|U\\|^{\kappa_n}-1)$, with tail expectation asymptotic to $3t^{\kappa_n}/(3-\kappa_n)$. At $t=n^{1/5}$, its bias grows as $\exp(\sqrt{\log n}/5)$, whereas the printed exponent term is only $O(\sqrt{\log n})$ and sampling fluctuations are negligible relative to the bias. Here $R_\alpha\to0$ and the relevant radial moments are finite. The false step linearizes $\\|U\\|^{\alpha-\hat\alpha_n}$ despite an unbounded product $R_\alpha\log t$.  (e) Set $\beta=3$, $\alpha=1$, $s=1/4$, $A=I_2/2$, and $Y(d)=d\\|U\\|$. Let $q_j=2^{4^j}$ and define a bounded nonincreasing function $b(q)=q_j^{-1/4}$ on $4q_{j-1}<q\leq4q_j$, extending it boundedly below the first interval. Fit $\hat p=3/5$, $\hat g(x,1,s)=1+b(n_t)$, and $\hat g(x,0,s)=0$. The assumptions hold surely with $R_p(n,\delta)=1/10+(1+n)^{-10}$ and $R_g(q,\delta)=b(q)+(1+q)^{-10}$. For $n_j=q_j^4$, $t_j=q_j/2$, the training count concentrates around $q_j$, because $\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>t)\sim t^{-3}/4$. Thus $b(n_t)=q_j^{-1/4}$ with probability tending to one, and the conditional DR mean is $1+b(n_t)/6$. But $n_jt_j^{-3}=8q_j$, where $b(8q_j)=q_{j+1}^{-1/4}=q_j^{-1}$. Every term in the claimed bound is $O(q_j^{-1/2})$, while the actual bias is $q_j^{-1/4}/6$. This disproves the replacement of the argument of $R_g$ using monotonicity alone.  (f) Finally, take $\beta=3$, $\alpha=1$, $s=1/4$, $A=I_2$, and $Y(d)=\\|U\\|(d+\epsilon)$ with independent standard normal $\epsilon$. Fix $\delta=1/10$. Let $B$ be the event that the first training observation has covariate below $1/10$. On $B^c$, fit the oracle nuisances; on $B$, fit $\hat p=3/5$, $\hat g(x,1,s)=2$, and $\hat g(x,0,s)=0$. At this confidence tolerance, take the small positive decreasing nuisance bounds above: their joint guarantee holds with probability exactly $9/10$. For smaller tolerances, bounds $1/10+(1+n)^{-10}$ and $1+(1+q)^{-10}$ cover the bad fit surely, so the assumption holds for every tolerance. At $t=n^{1/5}$, the bad-fit estimator converges to $1+1/6$, while on $B^c$ the scaled error $n^{1/5}(\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}-1)$ converges to a nondegenerate normal law. The printed right-hand side is a fixed multiple of $n^{-1/5}$. For every finite such multiple, limiting coverage is strictly below $9/10$: the bad branch fails, and the good branch has positive Gaussian failure probability. Consequently the same-$\delta$ confidence conclusion does not follow from the stated nuisance guarantee. minimal patch (confirmed): corrected statement: Under the assumption of Theorem \ref{thm:rate}, suppose in addition that the conditions of Proposition~\ref{prop:id} hold. Suppose that the fitted propensity, regression, and exponent are measurable with respect to the training split and independent auxiliary data. Suppose that the nuisance inequalities of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate} hold jointly with probability at least $1-\varepsilon$, uniformly almost surely over the covariate and direction support and both treatments, for each tolerance $\varepsilon$. Suppose that $0<p(X)<1$ almost surely and that the fitted-exponent pseudo-outcome has finite conditional absolute mean on those nuisance events. Put $\pi_t=\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>t)$ and $M=\|\mathcal I\|$, and set $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}=0$ when $M=0$. Let $\bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)$, let $C$ bound $\|g\|$ on its compact domain, and put $G_t=C+\bar e(t)$. For a generic evaluation observation define \[ \psi=\hat g(X,1,U/\\|U\\|)-\hat g(X,0,U/\\|U\\|)+\frac{D-\hat p(X)}{\hat p(X)(1-\hat p(X))}\left(\frac{Y}{\\|U\\|^{\hat\alpha_n}}-\hat g(X,D,U/\\|U\\|)\right). \] For any positive truncation level $B$ chosen using only the training split and independent auxiliary data, define \[ \begin{aligned} v_t(B)&=\mathbb E[\psi^2 I\{\|\psi\|\leq B\}\mid\mathcal D_1,\hbox{ auxiliary data},\\|U\\|>t],\\ a_t(B)&=\mathbb E[\|\psi\|I\{\|\psi\|>B\}\mid\mathcal D_1,\hbox{ auxiliary data},\\|U\\|>t]. \end{aligned} \] Fix $a_0\in(0,\beta)$ and write $a=R_\alpha(n,\delta/4)$. For sufficiently large $t\geq1$, if $a\leq a_0$ and $n\pi_t\geq16\log(4/\delta)$, then with probability at least $1-\delta$ we have $M\geq n\pi_t/4$ and \begin{align*} \|\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\tmop{DR}}-\eta\|\leq{}&\sqrt{\frac{4v_t(B)}{\delta M}}+\left(1+\frac4\delta\right)a_t(B)+\frac2c R_p(n/2,\delta/4)R_g(n_t,\delta/4)\\ &+2\bar e(t)+2L W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|})+\frac{2G_t}{c}\left(t^a-1+K a t^a\right), \end{align*} where $K$ depends on the radial law and $a_0$. Suppose moreover that the angular Wasserstein term satisfies the bound that Proposition 3.1 of \citet{zhang2023wasserstein} gives for this Pareto-type model, which is taken from that work and not proved here: there are constants $C_W>0$, $k\geq0$ and $t_W\geq1$ such that \[ W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|})\leq C_W(\log t)^k t^{-\min\{1,\beta\}}+C_W t^{-\beta s/(1-2s)}\qquad(t\geq t_W). \] Then for $t\geq t_W$ the angular term $2L W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|})$ of the display is at most $2LC_W(\log t)^k t^{-\min\{1,\beta\}}+2LC_W t^{-\beta s/(1-2s)}$, with the logarithmic factor retained. When the conditional score variance $V_t$ is finite, the sampling term may instead be taken to be $\sqrt{4V_t/(\delta M)}$. · proof change: Condition on the training split and independent auxiliary data, denoted together by $\mathcal T$. Write $R_U=\\|U\\|$, $S_U=U/\\|U\\|$, and \[ m_d^t(x,s)=\mathbb E[Y/R_U^{\alpha}\mid X=x,D=d,S_U=s,R_U>t],\qquad \eta_\alpha^t=\mathbb E[(f(X,1,U)-f(X,0,U))/R_U^{\alpha}\mid R_U>t]. \] Independence of $U$ from $(X,D)$ gives $\mathbb E[D\mid X,S_U,R_U>t]=p(X)$. Let $\psi_\alpha$ be the score $\psi$ with $\hat\alpha_n$ replaced by $\alpha$, keeping the same fitted functions. Direct conditional expectation over treatment and outcome gives \begin{align*} \mathbb E[\psi_\alpha\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]-\eta_\alpha^t=\mathbb E\left[(p(X)-\hat p(X))\left\{\frac{m_1^t(X,S_U)-\hat g(X,1,S_U)}{\hat p(X)}+\frac{m_0^t(X,S_U)-\hat g(X,0,S_U)}{1-\hat p(X)}\right\}\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t\right]. \end{align*} Indeed, the coefficients of $m_1^t-\hat g_1$ and $m_0^t-\hat g_0$ are respectively $p/\hat p-1$ and $1-(1-p)/(1-\hat p)$, which equal $(p-\hat p)/\hat p$ and $(p-\hat p)/(1-\hat p)$. On the joint nuisance event at tolerance $\delta/4$, the uniform bounds and estimated-propensity overlap therefore imply \[ \|\mathbb E[\psi_\alpha\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]-\eta_\alpha^t\|\leq\frac2c R_p(n/2,\delta/4)R_g(n_t,\delta/4). \]      where $n_t = \sum_{i = 1}^{n/2} I (\\|U_i \\|> t)$ is a random variable. Keep this training count as the argument of $R_g$; no regularity of that error function beyond its stated monotonicity is needed. Homogeneity at each realized radius gives \[ \|\eta_\alpha^t-\mathbb E[g(X,1,S_U)-g(X,0,S_U)\mid R_U>t]\|\leq2\bar e(t). \] The function $s\mapsto\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]$ is $2L$-Lipschitz. Independence of $X$ and $U$ and the definition of the limiting angular target therefore give \[ \|\eta_\alpha^t-\eta\|\leq2\bar e(t)+2L W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}). \] Proposition 3.1 of \cite{zhang2023wasserstein}, with Wasserstein order one and tail index $\beta$, supplies the stated angular bound with its logarithmic factors. Next, the fitted functions cancel from $\psi-\psi_\alpha$. Taking conditional expectation over the outcome and treatment leaves \[ \mathbb E\left[\left\{\frac{p(X)}{\hat p(X)}\frac{f(X,1,U)}{R_U^{\alpha}}-\frac{1-p(X)}{1-\hat p(X)}\frac{f(X,0,U)}{R_U^{\alpha}}\right\}\left(R_U^{\alpha-\hat\alpha_n}-1\right)\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t\right]. \] On the nuisance event, $\|\alpha-\hat\alpha_n\|\leq a$. Since $R_U\geq t\geq1$, we have $\|R_U^{\alpha-\hat\alpha_n}-1\|\leq R_U^a-1$. Homogeneity also gives $\|f(X,d,U)/R_U^{\alpha}\|\leq G_t$, so \[ \|\mathbb E[\psi-\psi_\alpha\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\|\leq\frac{2G_t}{c}\mathbb E[R_U^a-1\mid R_U>t]. \] Choose $\epsilon=(\beta-a_0)/2>0$. Potter's bound gives, uniformly for $r\geq1$ and sufficiently large $t$, \[ \frac{\mathbb P(R_U>tr)}{\pi_t}\leq2r^{-\beta+\epsilon}. \] Integrating the radial tail, for $0\leq a\leq a_0$, yields \begin{align*} \mathbb E[R_U^a-1\mid R_U>t]&=t^a-1+t^a\int_1^{\infty}a r^{a-1}\frac{\mathbb P(R_U>tr)}{\pi_t}\,\tmop{dr}\\ &\leq t^a-1+\frac{2a t^a}{\beta-\epsilon-a}\leq t^a-1+K a t^a. \end{align*} This also covers $a=0$, when both sides vanish. Combining the three mean-error bounds controls $\|\mathbb E[\psi\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]-\eta\|$ by the nonsampling terms in the statement. For evaluation, $M$ is binomial with parameters $n/2$ and $\pi_t$, independent of the training split and auxiliary data. The binomial lower-tail bound gives \[ \mathbb P(M<n\pi_t/4)\leq\exp(-n\pi_t/16)\leq\delta/4. \] Conditional on $\mathcal T$ and any positive $M$, the evaluation scores are independent with the conditional tail law used to define $v_t(B)$ and $a_t(B)$. Put $Z=\psi I\{\|\psi\|\leq B\}$ and $W=\psi I\{\|\psi\|>B\}$. Chebyshev's inequality gives \[ \mathbb P\left(\left\|\frac1M\sum Z_i-\mathbb E[Z\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\right\|>\sqrt{\frac{4v_t(B)}{\delta M}}\ \middle\|\ \mathcal T,M\right)\leq\delta/4. \] Markov's inequality gives \[ \mathbb P\left(\frac1M\sum\|W_i\|>\frac{4a_t(B)}\delta\ \middle\|\ \mathcal T,M\right)\leq\delta/4; \] if $a_t(B)=0$, the tail variables vanish almost surely and this assertion holds directly. On the intersection of these events, the triangle inequality gives \[ \left\|\frac1M\sum\psi_i-\mathbb E[\psi\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\right\|\leq\sqrt{\frac{4v_t(B)}{\delta M}}+\left(1+\frac4\delta\right)a_t(B). \] The joint nuisance event fails with probability at most $\delta/4$. Adding its failure probability, the count failure probability, and the two conditional sampling failure probabilities gives total failure probability at most $\delta$. The estimator is the average of these scores on the resulting event, which proves the bound. If $V_t<\infty$, apply Chebyshev directly to the untruncated score average with failure probability $\delta/4$; this gives the stated replacement sampling term, with total failure probability still at most $\delta$. · narrowed: A finite universal square-root rate for all integrable scores, bounds using only $e(t)$, a logarithm-free angular bias at $\beta=1$, uniform linear dependence on $R_\alpha\log t$, replacement of the random training count inside arbitrary decreasing $R_g$, and confidence $1-\delta$ with unchanged nuisance arguments. The suggested conditional evaluation argument also excludes fits that use the evaluation observations and fits whose nuisance guarantees hold only at a random evaluation point; its nonvacuous bound requires an integrable fitted-exponent pseudo-outcome and an exponent-error bound smaller than $\beta$. Cases in which conditional mean exogeneity given $(X,U)$ fails, or in which either potential outcome has an infinite absolute tail mean, are also excluded. The rate of the angular Wasserstein term is taken from Proposition 3.1 of \citet{zhang2023wasserstein} as an explicit assumption, with its poly-logarithmic factor, and is not proved. |

But $\eta=1$ and every term in the printed bound tends to zero. The omitted term in the conditional mean is $\mathbb E[\\|U\\|^{\alpha-\hat\alpha_n}-1\mid\\|U\\|>t]$. (b) Take $\beta=2$, $\alpha=\hat\alpha_n=0$, $Y(d)=d+E$, $g=\hat g=d$, and $e=0$, with independent centered symmetric stable noise $E$ of index $6/5$. At $t=n^{1/6}$ it has scale $n^{-1/9}$ while the printed bound is of order $n^{-1/6}$; failure probability tends to one. With sample sizes of order $t_j^{10}$, sampling error is negligible and the printed right-hand side is of order $t_j^{-1}$. Thus the claimed approximation-bias step is false. (d) Take $\beta=1$, $\alpha=\hat\alpha_n=0$, $Y(d)=d(1+\min\{U_1,U_2\}/\\|U\\|)$, exact regression, and $e=0$. The limiting angular contrast is $1$. Restricting integration to $U_1\in[t,2t]$, $U_2\in[1,t/2]$ gives $\mathbb E[\min\{U_1,U_2\}/\\|U\\|\mid\\|U\\|>t]\geq C(\log t)/t$ for some $C>0$. With sample sizes of order $t^6$, the displayed bound is only of order $t^{-1}$. minimal patch (confirmed): corrected statement: Under the assumption of Theorem \ref{thm:rate}, suppose in addition that the conditions of Proposition~\ref{prop:id} hold. Suppose in addition that the conditions of Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} hold. Use its training split and independent auxiliary data, denoted together by $\mathcal T$, its $\pi_t=\mathbb P(\\|U\\|>t)$, $M=\|\mathcal I\|$, $\bar e(t)=\sup_{r\geq t}e(r)$, and $G_t=C+\bar e(t)$, where $C$ bounds $\|g\|$ on its compact domain. Set $\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\text{IPW}}=0$ if $M=0$. For a generic evaluation observation define \[ \phi=\frac{Y}{\\|U\\|^{\hat\alpha_n}}\left(\frac{D}{\hat p(X)}-\frac{1-D}{1-\hat p(X)}\right).\] For any $B>0$ chosen using only $\mathcal T$, put \[\begin{aligned} v_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)&=\mathbb E[\phi^2 I\{\|\phi\|\leq B\}\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t],\\ b_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)&=\mathbb E[\|\phi\|I\{\|\phi\|>B\}\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t].\end{aligned}\] Fix $a_0\in(0,\beta)$ and put $a=R_\alpha(n,\delta/4)$. For sufficiently large $t\geq1$, if $a\leq a_0$ and $n\pi_t\geq16\log(4/\delta)$, then with probability at least $1-\delta$ we have $M\geq n\pi_t/4$ and \[\begin{aligned} \|\hat\eta_{n,t}^{\text{IPW}}-\eta\|\leq{}&\sqrt{\frac{4v_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)}{\delta M}}+\left(1+\frac4\delta\right)b_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)+\frac{2G_t}{c}R_p(n/2,\delta/4)\\ &+2\bar e(t)+2L W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|})+\frac{2G_t}{c}(t^a-1+K a t^a),\end{aligned}\] where $K$ depends on the radial law and $a_0$. Under the angular assumption of Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate}, taken from Proposition 3.1 of \citet{zhang2023wasserstein}, the angular Wasserstein term has the bound stated there for $t\geq t_W$, with its logarithmic factor retained. When $V_t^{\text{IPW}}=\tmop{Var}(\phi\mid\mathcal T,\\|U\\|>t)$ is finite, the sampling term may instead be taken to be $\sqrt{4V_t^{\text{IPW}}/(\delta M)}$. · proof change: Write $R_U=\\|U\\|$ and $S_U=U/\\|U\\|$, and define \[\begin{aligned} m_d^t(x,s)&=\mathbb E[Y/R_U^\alpha\mid X=x,D=d,S_U=s,R_U>t],\\ \eta_\alpha^t&=\mathbb E[(f(X,1,U)-f(X,0,U))/R_U^\alpha\mid R_U>t].\end{aligned}\] Independence of $U$ from $(X,D)$ gives $\mathbb P(D=1\mid X,S_U,R_U>t)=p(X)$. Let $\phi_\alpha$ be $\phi$ with $\hat\alpha_n$ replaced by $\alpha$. Conditional expectation over treatment and outcome gives \[\begin{aligned} &\mathbb E[\phi_\alpha\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]-\eta_\alpha^t\\ &\quad=\mathbb E\left[(p(X)-\hat p(X))\left\{\frac{m_1^t(X,S_U)}{\hat p(X)}+\frac{m_0^t(X,S_U)}{1-\hat p(X)}\right\}\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t\right].\end{aligned}\] Indeed, the treatment coefficients after subtraction are $p/\hat p-1=(p-\hat p)/\hat p$ and $1-(1-p)/(1-\hat p)=(p-\hat p)/(1-\hat p)$. Homogeneity gives $\|m_d^t\|\leq G_t$. On the joint nuisance event at tolerance $\delta/4$, estimated-propensity overlap therefore gives \[ \|\mathbb E[\phi_\alpha\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]-\eta_\alpha^t\|\leq\frac{2G_t}{c}R_p(n/2,\delta/4).\] Taking conditional expectations over the outcome in the exponent difference gives \[\begin{aligned} &\mathbb E[\phi-\phi_\alpha\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\\ &=\mathbb E\left[\left\{\frac{p(X)f(X,1,U)}{\hat p(X)R_U^\alpha}-\frac{(1-p(X))f(X,0,U)}{(1-\hat p(X))R_U^\alpha}\right\}(R_U^{\alpha-\hat\alpha_n}-1)\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t\right].\end{aligned}\] Since $R_U>t\geq1$ and $\|\alpha-\hat\alpha_n\|\leq a$, the absolute value is at most $(2G_t/c)\mathbb E[R_U^a-1\mid R_U>t]$. Put $\epsilon=(\beta-a_0)/2$. Potter's bound gives $\mathbb P(R_U>tr)/\pi_t\leq2r^{-\beta+\epsilon}$ for all $r\geq1$ and sufficiently large $t$. Integration gives \[\begin{aligned} \mathbb E[R_U^a-1\mid R_U>t]&=t^a-1+t^a\int_1^\infty a r^{a-1}\frac{\mathbb P(R_U>tr)}{\pi_t}\,\tmop{dr}\\ &\leq t^a-1+\frac{2a t^a}{\beta-\epsilon-a}\leq t^a-1+K a t^a,\end{aligned}\] with $K=4/(\beta-a_0)$; for $a=0$ both sides vanish. The triangle inequality now supplies the decomposition into sampling fluctuation, propensity error, exponent error, and $\|\eta_\alpha^t-\eta\|$.  Homogeneity at the realized radius gives \[ \|\eta_\alpha^t-\mathbb E[g(X,1,S_U)-g(X,0,S_U)\mid R_U>t]\|\leq2\bar e(t).\] The function $s\mapsto\mathbb E_X[g(X,1,s)-g(X,0,s)]$ is $2L$-Lipschitz. Independence of $X$ and $U$ and the Wasserstein coupling inequality therefore give \[ \|\eta_\alpha^t-\eta\|\leq2\bar e(t)+2L W_1(\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}^t,\mathcal L_{U/\\|U\\|}).\] Lemma~\ref{lemma:eta_rate} supplies the angular bound under the identical model assumptions, including its logarithmic factors. Conditional on $\mathcal T$ and positive $M$, the evaluation scores are independent with the conditional exceedance law. Put $Z=\phi I\{\|\phi\|\leq B\}$ and $W=\phi I\{\|\phi\|>B\}$. Chebyshev's and Markov's inequalities give, respectively, conditional failure probabilities at most $\delta/4$ for \[\begin{aligned} \left\|M^{-1}\sum Z_i-\mathbb E[Z\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\right\|&\leq\sqrt{4v_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)/(\delta M)},\\ M^{-1}\sum\|W_i\|&\leq4b_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)/\delta.\end{aligned}\] If $b_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)=0$, the second inequality holds almost surely because $W=0$ almost surely. Combining them with $\|\mathbb E[W\mid\mathcal T,R_U>t]\|\leq b_t^{\text{IPW}}(B)$ bounds the sampling fluctuation by the first two terms in the statement. The count has law $M\sim\tmop{Bin}(n/2,\pi_t)$, so \[ \mathbb P(M<n\pi_t/4)\leq\exp(-n\pi_t/16)\leq\delta/4.\] The joint nuisance event also fails with probability at most $\delta/4$. The union of these four failures has probability at most $\delta$. On their complement, the mean-error bounds and the sampling inequality prove the result. If $V_t^{\text{IPW}}<\infty$, direct conditional Chebyshev gives sampling error at most $\sqrt{4V_t^{\text{IPW}}/(\delta M)}$ with failure probability at most $\delta/4$, and the same union bound proves the asserted alternative. · narrowed: The universal square-root sampling rate for integrable scores of infinite variance, the omission of exponent error, the pointwise approximation term $e(t)$, the logarithm-free angular bound at $\beta=1$, nonuniform nuisance guarantees, fits using evaluation observations, degenerate true propensities, fitted-exponent pseudo-outcomes without finite absolute tail means, exponent-error bounds not below a fixed $a_0<\beta$, and thresholds with insufficient expected exceedance counts. Cases in which conditional mean exogeneity given $(X,U)$ fails, or in which either potential outcome has an infinite absolute tail mean, are also excluded. |

| statement | \label{cor:rate_ipw}Under the assumptions of Theorem~\ref{thm:rate}, further suppose that \begin{align*} R_p (n, \delta) = \Theta (\log (1 / \delta) n^{- 1 / 2}) , R_g (n, \delta) = \Theta (\log (1 / \delta) n^{- 1 / 2}), R_\alpha (n, \delta) = \Theta( \log(1/\delta) n^{-c_\alpha}), \end{align*} for some $c_\alpha > 0 $, the following conclusions hold. \begin{enumerate} \item If $s \in (0, 1 / (2 + \max \{1, \beta\}))$, takes $t_n = \Theta (n^{ (1 - 2 s) \hat{\gamma}_{n}})$, with probability at least $1 - \delta$, we have \begin{align*} \| {{\widehat{\theta }_{n, t}^{\text{IPW} }} } - \theta^{NETE} \| & = O (e (t_n) + n^{- s} \log (1 / \delta) + n^{-c_\alpha} \log(n)\log (1 / \delta)) . \end{align*} \item If $s \in [1 / (2 + \max \{1, \beta\}), 1 / 2)$, \ takes $t = \Theta (n^{(\hat{\gamma}_{n} / (1 + 2 \min \{1, \hat{\gamma}_{n} \})})$, with probability at least $1 - \delta$, we have \begin{align*} \| {{\widehat{\theta }_{n, t}^{\text{IPW} }} } - \theta^{NETE} \| & = O (e (t_n) + n^{- 1 / (2 + \max \{\beta, 1\})} \log (1 / \delta) + n^{-c_\alpha} \log(n)\log (1 / \delta)) . \end{align*} \end{enumerate} |
